Goodbye, GrubHub. Amazon Prime Has a New Meal Delivery Partner: Uber Eats

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

The switch comes with a limited-time offer.

Your Amazon Prime membership has a new perk — and it’s available now.

On Thursday, Amazon announced that Prime members in the US will get free delivery on Uber Eats orders made directly through Amazon’s app or website. And you’ll get $20 off the first two Uber Eats eligible orders of $25 or more for the first 14 days, plus Amazon-exclusive offers. If you have a Prime Visa credit card, you’ll earn 5% back on all Uber Eats orders until Dec. 5, with a Prime membership.

It’s another perk that may make Amazon Prime membership worthwhile, especially if you already use Uber Eats. But you may wonder what that means for the GrubHub partnership that both Amazon and GrubHub touted for years — dating back to 2022. Before now, your Prime subscription included a GrubHub Plus membership at no additional cost. GrubHub Plus included no delivery fees, lower service fees and 5% back on pickup orders.

The new partnership launch just so happens to be the same day that Lyft and DoorDash announced that they’re teaming up for a new subscription, Lyft Go. An Amazon spokesperson said in an email to CNET that the GrubHub and Amazon partnership ended on Thursday. There’s no telling how long Amazon will partner with Uber Eats.

“We continuously evaluate our third-party offers to ensure they provide the best possible value for Prime members,” the spokesperson said. “As part of that ongoing evaluation, we identified an opportunity to deliver an even better restaurant delivery experience.”

However, a GrubHub spokesperson said in an email to CNET that if you have a Prime membership and activated GrubHub Plus, you’ll have the membership until Dec. 31, 2027.

GrubHub shared a joint statement from Amazon and GrubHub in an email to CNET:

On Nov. 5, Grubhub and Amazon have decided to end the Prime-exclusive Grubhub Plus offer, and Amazon customers will no longer be able to place Grubhub orders through Amazon.com or the Amazon Shopping app. Amazon and Grubhub are proud of the partnership and the value provided to Prime members over the years. As the agreement concludes, both companies remain committed to serving our customers and delivering a great customer experience.

The new Uber Eats perk is now available to all Prime members, including those with Prime Access and Prime for Young Adults. And if you’re looking for another dining option, your Amazon Prime membership still offers free same-day delivery on perishable grocery orders over $25 when you order from Amazon Grocery.

Di’Shon Bernard fit but three players still out as Dave Challinor issues Sheffield Wednesday injury latest

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Sheffield Wednesday face Oldham Athletic in the FA Cup on Sunday as Dave Challinor takes charge of his new side for the first time. The new SWFC boss has a squad full of talented players to pick from, but he has injuries to consider too.

And, speaking to the press ahead of his maiden game, Challinor confirmed that trio Liam Cooper, Joe Lumley and Ali Al-Hamadi are set to remain out. Cooper is more of a precautionary decision, while Lumley and Al-Hamadi are targeting returns to training next week.

In better news, Di’Shon Bernard is fit and available having trained all week. Challinor and the Owls will remain cautious with his load management after a long time on the sidelines.

Cup matches against opponents in lower divisions often offers teams the chance to rotate, giving fringe players the chance to come in and impress. It will be intriguing to see just what sort of approach Challinor takes though, as he gets to grips with his Sheffield Wednesday squad.

Early on, Challinor has to determine his strongest starting lineup. The most efficient way to do so will be by playing as strong a side as possible in this FA Cup clash with Oldham Athletic.

Alternatively, Challinor will have done his research before taking over at SWFC, and he watched the Blackpool draw. If he already has a good idea of his favoured XI, he can take this game to give chances to those projected to be on the fringes to get a clearer picture of the full squad at his disposal.

It is an interesting dilemma for Challinor, and it will be interesting to see how he approaches it.

Lumley’s continued absence means Ashley Maynard-Brewer will likely keep his place in goal. Cooper being on the sidelines gives Bernard a good chance of coming straight into the side as well.

Challinor had a strong squad at his disposal at Stockport County, but it feels like this Sheffield Wednesday group is a level up. There are experienced pros like Cooper, Barry Bannan and Liam Palmer, alongside players who could arguably be at a higher level, such as Callum Slattery, Billy Mitchell and defender Bernard.

Pair this with the youthful exuberance of Ernie Weaver, Will Grainger and Harry Gray, and SWFC have a squad built for the short and long-term. There’s a good balance of profiles and characters, making up a recipe for success at Hillsborough.

Challinor faces plenty of selection headaches in deciding his best lineup, and he will be excited to find it out.

Longtime investor Martin Tobias says no to 99% of startups — his new AI tool helps them find a yes

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Chronicling the Seattle and Pacific Northwest startup scene. SEE MORE

by Kurt Schlosser on Nov 5, 2026 at 7:01 amNovember 5, 2026 at 7:01 am

Martin Tobias was pitched by about 4,500 startups last year. He wrote 10 checks.

Now the pre-seed investor is offering the rest of them a consolation prize: an AI tool that tells founders which investors are most likely to say yes.

Tobias, a longtime figure in the Seattle tech scene, is now based in Spokane, Wash. He founded Loudeye, a digital media company that went public during the dot-com boom and later sold to Nokia, and went on to lead biofuel maker Imperium Renewables and group-buying startup Tippr.

He was a venture partner at Ignition Partners, an early backer of DocuSign, before starting Incisive Ventures in 2020. The firm closed a $10.5 million fund last year to back early-stage B2B software companies.

Investor Match started as an internal tool to help Incisive’s portfolio companies find seed and Series A investors. Tobias said he has 17,000 contacts but can only keep about 150 of them in his head, a limit known as Dunbar’s number.

To use the tool, founders upload a pitch deck, and Investor Match returns 20 investors ranked by fit, based on stage, check size, geography and what each firm has actually backed. It also drafts outreach emails, but it doesn’t send them.

Most founders send those emails cold, or pass the list to their existing investors and ask for warm introductions, Tobias said. Investors who have accounts on the platform can also respond to founders directly.

A free preview shows all 20 matches with fit scores, with the top three fully expanded. The full list costs $20, which Tobias said roughly covers the API costs of running it, about $1,000 a month.

Every founder Tobias turns down now gets pointed to the tool.

“Just because I wasn’t the right investor for that company doesn’t mean that the right investor wasn’t out there,” he told GeekWire.

Incisive appears on the platform like any other investor. Some founders who were matched with the firm asked whether Tobias had tilted the algorithm toward himself. He said he hadn’t, and that he only wants pitches that actually fit.

“Just because I wasn’t the right investor for that company doesn’t mean that the right investor wasn’t out there.”

Other companies offer investor matching as part of broader fundraising platforms, including Metal and OpenVC, which Tobias said charge founders monthly subscriptions. He said OpenVC relies on simple keyword matching, while Investor Match uses AI to read each investor’s website, portfolio and blog posts, which takes “millions and millions of tokens.”

Some of those companies are trying to build venture-scale businesses off founders’ fundraising, Tobias said.

“I think that’s just f—ing evil,” he said. “I don’t think you should try to make money off people who quit their job and are putting everything into trying to raise their first one to $2 million.”

Founders have told Tobias they’ve raised more than $100 million from investors they found through the platform since it launched about six months ago, though he acknowledged he can’t verify that because the money doesn’t flow through Investor Match.

Investor Match is also an extension of his investing thesis. He said his fund looks for B2B software companies like DocuSign that “reduce friction at scale” by taking a business process and doing it better in software.

“That’s exactly what Investor Match is,” he said. “What’s the friction in the market that you’re dealing with? Investors finding founders, founders finding investors. OK, can software help with that? I think it can.”

Swallowed by the Camino Green Tunnel

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The room we slept in was the top floor of a centuries-old, three-story building with a modern interior. Tuck-pointed right under the roof, we slept soundly through the night as periodic thunder and a heavy rain provided peaceful white noise overhead.

Stepping outside the next morning, sunrise illuminated a surreal sky, backlighting the massive monastery. We paused at a local cafe for a quick breakfast before leaving Samos behind.

Our hike started with a few miles of road walk before the Camino veered onto a forested, ancient track. Thick rock walls lined the path, guarded by groves of massive chestnut trees.

As the morning sun broke through, the air naturally turned humid. We began a steady climb along a narrow path where pilgrims over the years had woven stick crosses directly into the fencing alongside the trail.

Highlight of the Day: We ran into Anthony from Oxford–who was walking his fifth Camino. He explained that this sunken section of path is called a Holloway. Worn deep into the earth over centuries of foot traffic, it creates a stunning green tunnel with 12-foot mossy walls and a dense canopy of ancient trees overhead.

Our “tramily” friends, Jill and Robert, caught up with us midmorning, and the miles flew by as we chatted. We meandered from one quaint hamlet to the next along quite country roads. By 10:30 AM, we only had 2.5 miles left for the day.

We soon reached Foxos, a tiny village with cute medieval farm houses, old barns, and a historic church. From there, the path climbed steeply toward Aguiada, where our trail merged back on the main Camino Frances route. We were completely out of water, but this small village didn’t have any water sources available.

Reconnecting with the main route brought a dramatic shift. Because you only need to walk the final 100 km to earn a Compostela certificate, and since it was the start of the weekend, the trail was suddenly packed with weekend hikers and new pilgrims starting their journey from Sarria.

It instantly felt like a different Camino–less intimate, much more crowded, and distinctly touristy.

We rolled into Sarria at 1:00 PM. Built into a hillside, Sarria is a charming medieval city full of narrow, winding cobblestone streets.

Our accommodation was easily one of our favorites so far:

While Knockers immediately crashed for a two-hour nap, I got to work planning our journey past Santiago.

Next Steps: We officially decided that after reaching Santiago, we will hike out to Finisterre—traditionally known as “the end of the world” on the Atlantic coast. From there, we’ll return to Santiago and head south along the Portuguese Caminos. (There are a few ferry connections and complex logistics to map out for that stretch!)

Later that evening, we walked downhill to a local grocery store and definitely bought too much food. Being hungry and finding fresh, real ingredients for the first time in a long while made it impossible to resist.

Back at the apartment, we cooked up a huge meal and ate until we couldn’t take another bite. By the time “hiker midnight” rolled around, we crashed hard for another peaceful night’s rest.

After decades of no backpacking an abrupt life incident forced me back into the woods for some shinrin-yoku where I ran into a NOBO tramily at Shuckstack Fire Tower. That started an idea of a longer term forest bath on the AT. What a long strange trip its been. Any financial donations I get will be used to send out love to other thru hikers a trail magic.

SHT Day 14: The Nearly Unfortunate Incident

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Last night was my final night spent in my tent. For the remainder of this year’s SHT adventure I will be sleeping in my camper. I heard coyotes at one point in the early evening, and also a train all night long. It didn’t blare its horn all the time, but I definitely heard the constant hum of the motor.

I knew I had shorter mileage today, with my husband likely arriving later in the afternoon, so I was yet again in no hurry to leave camp. I stretched out and took off around 9:20. The morning was a quiet, lonely hike, passing by Beaver Pond and Fault Line campsites without seeing anyone.

The farther north I go, the more the trees have changed color.

I had planned to unpack my stove and take a legitimate coffee break at Fault Line, but opted instead to just shake up the Trader Joe’s instant coffee with cold water when I filtered up at a stream. I do love a hot coffee on a cold morning, but I can be kinda lazy and I prefer not to stop for long once I’m in a groove. I definitely could have fired up the stove before I left camp, but I already had my breakfast shake. Besides, having this “cold brew” mid-morning was a nice jolt and change from plain water.

The trail did some steep ups and downs through what seemed like prime moose habitat before descending into the town of Beaver Bay. I crossed Lax Lake Road and kept going. I had service and talked to Bill (the husband), who said his ETA would just about match mine at the trailhead outside the next town of Silver Bay, another three or four trail miles for me.

This is one of my favorite pics. It would be top ten if there was a moose down there!

After Lax Lake Road I started to see more people. I passed by the Beaver River campsites, the second of which would have been an amazing spot to stay. Instead, I had L&L here, and stayed just long enough to appreciate its beauty. The latrine seemed private enough, as it was set back in the woods, away from the river and tent spots. Just after I finished my business, I heard an ATV coming closer. Turns out there was a trail, mere feet behind the latrine! I finished in the nick of time!

The driver didn’t seem to notice me, or at least knew the trail well enough to expect someone may be on the throne and look away. This is the second time I’ve noticed a latrine well within sight of passers-by. The first was at East Gooseberry Campsite, where it was I who happened to see an unfortunate person doing their business on the latrine, as it was not very hidden at all. I screeched a quick “I’m so sorry!” as I looked away, feeling terrible for the person caught with their literal pants down. Let’s just say some latrine spots out here are better than others.

Day hikers took my picture on the Beaver River bridge.

The view from near one of the Beaver River campsites.

After the close call, I headed on. The trail crossed a few roads and came very close to the golf course in Silver Bay. It also briefly followed the Gitchi-Gami trail, a well-established and paved trail for non-motorized use. There were a few final steep climbs and descents over rocky cliffs when I suddenly saw Bill on trail! He came up to meet me about a quarter mile from the Penn Boulevard trailhead. We finished the steep downhill together and crossed Penn Boulevard to reach the massive trailhead, where the sweet sight of our truck and camper was waiting for me.

The Gitchi-Gami crossing under the train tracks.

Bill had an electrolyte drink ready for me, and I stretched while we talked trail news. This trailhead, the largest I’ve seen for the SHT so far, is just on the outskirts of Silver Bay. It must be popular!

Now we’re off to Lamb’s Resort, near the town of Schroeder, for the next few days. This campground will serve as our first home base as I continue to tick off trail miles as a series of day hikes. Having completed nearly 100 miles so far, I’m ready for a zero day, a shower, and a bed with a snuggly electric blanket.

My name is Misty, and I hail from Nebraska, where I live with my husband, two cats, and flock of chickens. I worked in healthcare for over 16 years before giving it up for more outdoor pursuits. I hope to have you follow along on my new adventures!

How To Optimize For Better Campaign Leads, Not Just Conversions – Ask A PPC via @sejournal, @navahf

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: 9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

Improve lead quality by feeding qualification and revenue data back to ad platforms, so automated bidding can prioritize business outcomes over form fills.

This month, a really good question came in:

“My campaigns are generating plenty of leads and hitting our CPA target, but the sales team says the quality is getting worse. How can I optimize for better leads rather than just more conversions?”

The short answer is: If you want better leads, you first need to teach the platform what a better lead looks like.

It’s very easy to lose track of real-world performance when platform-reported performance looks great. Your CPA may be within target and lead volume may look healthy, while your sales team is telling you those leads aren’t turning into meaningful opportunities.

Those two things aren’t necessarily contradictory.

The ad platform may actually be doing exactly what you asked it to do: finding more of the conversions you told it were valuable. If every form fill is treated as the same conversion equally valuable, the bidding system doesn’t inherently know that one became a strong sales opportunity and another went nowhere.

That means improving lead quality starts with the definition of success you’re giving the platform.

In this post, we’ll look at how to define a better outcome, pass that information back to the platform, and give automated bidding enough meaningful signal and time to optimize toward it.

A quick disclosure: I am a Microsoft Advertising employee, and while I’ll reference Microsoft Advertising capabilities where they’re useful, these principles can apply to paid media programs across platforms.

Before changing your bidding strategy or implementing a new measurement tool, ask a more fundamental question:

What actually makes one lead better than another for your business?

For some advertisers, the initial form fill may be meaningful enough. For others, a more granular segmentation might be needed:

The goal should be to get as close as you reasonably can to the deepest meaningful funnel outcome you can reliably measure.

If 100 leads came through but only 10 were SQLs, you don’t want to teach the bidding system that all 100 were equally valuable simply because they completed the same form.

The closer your conversion tracking gets to the outcome the business actually cares about, the more meaningful the information you’re giving the bidding system.

That’s also why marketing and sales alignment matters here. If the platform says CPA is great while Sales says lead quality is deteriorating, both things can be true.

Before optimizing for “better leads,” make sure everyone agrees on what better actually means. Check that your Sales team is using current lead quality to inform their decisions rather than applying historical bias, as lead quality can change and improve over time with campaign optimizations.

See also: The Conversion Setup Errors That Break Smart Bidding

Once you’ve defined the outcome, the next step is giving the platform information that helps distinguish higher-value actions from lower-value ones.

This is where real-world qualification and revenue data become particularly useful. The best signal any ad platform can work off of is conversion values, and passing accurate values will ensure budgets are directed effectively. This is why conversion value rules are so effective: by adapting the conversion value based on probability of value, you can make it even easier for ad platforms to deliver the right leads.

If you aren’t going to use real-world numbers or outcomes, your CPA or ROAS goals may need to be treated as directional guidance rather than representations of actual business results.

There will also be cases where your ultimate outcome doesn’t happen frequently enough to give conversion-based bidding sufficient volume and signal to effectively optimize performance.

That’s where an earlier but still meaningful conversion can help. A lead becoming marketing-qualified, sales-qualified, or progressing further into your funnel tells you more about lead quality than an unrelated engagement metric.

A useful benchmark for automated bidding is at least 30 conversions within 30 days. Following significant changes to budgets or targets, conversion-based bidding can take as many as roughly 50 conversions to calibrate according to multiple platforms.

See also: Ask A PPC: What Are Learning Periods In Digital Marketing?

Once you know which outcomes matter, you need a way to pass that information back to the platform.

If you can’t yet measure true value, identify the most meaningful signal you can track reliably and use conversion value rules to assign it directional value. If you’re able to measure and optimize against real value, use offline conversion imports to bring that data into your ads platform. This can fill in the gaps so platforms optimize against real value.

That’s where mechanisms such as offline conversions and Conversions APIs come in.

Offline conversions let you connect outcomes that happen after the immediate website experience back to the original ad interaction. Think about someone who clicks an ad, fills out a form, talks to a sales contact, and eventually becomes a customer.

Microsoft Advertising, for example, documents the Click ID, conversion time, and conversion goal name among the core information used for offline conversion imports.

And Conversions API, commonly called CAPI, lets conversion information move programmatically from your systems into the ad platform rather than relying exclusively on periodic manual uploads.

Microsoft Advertising’s Conversions API lets advertisers send conversion and engagement events directly from their servers using Universal Event Tracking (UET), and can pass business signals including revenue, product details, click IDs, hashed identifiers, and consent status.

The mechanism isn’t the strategy. The quality of the signal you’re sending through it is.

Before automating the flow of conversion data, make sure you trust the data you’re automating.

If your CRM is treating poor-quality leads as successful outcomes, passing that information back faster won’t solve the problem. You’ll simply create a more efficient feedback loop around the wrong definition of success.

This also applies to gaps in volume or value of conversions. Advertisers should confirm where the gap is appearing:

Auditing for these factors, as much as the technical passing through of conversion data, is a critical step in closing the loop.

See also: Is Your Conversion Data Misleading You? Seven Common Google Ads Tracking Issues

There is one more piece that can easily get overlooked: conversion delay.

Your campaign may look one way when you initially review results and very different after enough time has passed for leads to move through the sales process.

Microsoft Advertising conversion tracking supports configurable conversion windows, depending on the goal, from one minute up to 90 days.

CAPI introduces another consideration. On Microsoft Advertising, CAPI currently has a 90-day look-back window. Consider which outcomes realistically happen soon enough to pass back through that mechanism.

Your systems still need time for sales to work the lead or for a customer to actually convert.

Your reporting cadence should reflect your actual sales cycle, and automated bidding needs enough meaningful data and time to learn.

That may mean resisting the instinct to make aggressive changes because yesterday’s platform numbers don’t yet contain outcomes that take weeks to materialize.

Everything above assumes the campaign’s purpose is ultimately a conversion-driven business outcome.

If you’re running an awareness or consideration campaign, it may generate sales and booked demos, but these should be reported as secondary key performance indicators rather than the appropriate immediate measures of success. Instead of forcing every campaign into the same CPA or ROAS framework, start by being explicit about the business outcome the campaign is intended to drive and select measurement signals appropriate to that objective.

The important thing is consistency between what you want the campaign to accomplish and what you’re asking the platform to optimize toward.

Conversion totals also don’t explain everything happening between an ad interaction and the eventual outcome.

That’s where diagnostic signals can be useful.

Search behavior can help you understand whether you’re creating interest. Search term reporting, for example, can surface changes in branded searches or the kinds of queries coming from your audience.

Microsoft Clarity can help diagnose what happens after the click. Behavioral analytics such as recordings, heatmaps, and engagement insights from your website can help surface whether visitors are meaningfully exploring the site, encountering friction, or leaving without taking the intended action. Clarity AI Visibility can add another discovery signal by showing when content is cited in supported AI-generated experiences and which AI grounding queries triggered those citations.

These signals aren’t substitutes for your business outcome.

Instead, use them to help answer questions the conversion number alone can’t: Are we creating interest? Where are we losing it? Is campaign performance the problem, or is something happening later in the customer journey?

If you want better leads, don’t just ask your ad platform for more conversions. Teach it what a better conversion looks like.

Start by defining the deepest meaningful outcome you can reliably measure. Then pass qualification, revenue, or other value signals back where appropriate, so the platform has a better understanding of what success means to your business.

Use offline conversions, CAPI, or other feedback mechanisms to connect those real-world outcomes with advertising activity. And make sure you’re giving automated bidding enough meaningful signal and enough time to learn from it.

If you choose not to pass those real-world outcomes back, that’s valid too. Just be honest about what your platform metrics represent and keep your internal source of truth at the center of your business decisions.

The platform can only optimize toward the definition of success you give it.

So when lead volume looks great, but quality doesn’t, don’t immediately ask the bidding system to find more.

First, make sure you’ve taught it what “better” means.

Les Tells Designs Handbags Around the Body, Not Just the Outfit

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Les Tells founder Brianna Tell’s work is best explained by the intersections that inspire it. There’s the influence of her hometown of Lancaster, Pennsylvania, and her professional experience in Milan, Italy. There’s the intersection of experimental design and generations-old Italian craftsmanship. But perhaps the most important intersection is the one between bag and body. Where rigid corners and bulky silhouettes can cause discomfort, Tell wanted to explore what physical cohesion between bag and body could look like—a piece that functions not simply as an accessory, but as an extension of the wearer’s silhouette.

The result is the first collection from Les Tells: six luxury Italian-made bags and accessories designed to fit and complement the wearer’s body. Released on Nov. 3, the collection features four bags, a wallet, and a wristlet designed to follow the body’s natural curves. The idea was that an accessory could be luxurious and distinctive without the discomfort and awkwardness that can accompany high-concept design.

The idea came to Tell while she was visiting Paris. “I was watching all of these beautiful French people walking down the street as they are, and I just saw all of them in these beautiful outfits,” Tell recalls. “But then I saw some of the bags that they were wearing, and it just felt like they were sticking out from their body. That was the moment that I was like, why aren’t there bags that move better with your body?”

From there, she began the design process, sketching out silhouettes for crossbody bags and handbags. The concepts were distinctive, but the true test would come in how they moved with the body.

Tell’s approach to testing is, admittedly, a bit unconventional. Knowing the importance of how the pieces worked with a body in motion, she constructed paper models, testing out the bags’ mobility and identifying points of friction. The approach had its initial skeptics.

“I showed up with these paper models and [my friend] was like, ‘Nobody’s going to take you seriously. This is something that is not professional,'” she recalls. “But I still believed in this, and I wanted to keep on going. I went down and talked to those ateliers.”

She found a collaborator in a family-run atelier in Scandicci, near Florence. “They looked at me like I built something so different and unique that it really verified my gut feeling that I was on the right track.”

Over two years, she refined the designs. The Alexandras, a cylindrical bag with a detachable strap, has an inward curve designed to nestle comfortably against the wearer’s hip. The Logans, featuring an asymmetrical, oblong silhouette, is a crossbody bag designed to sit comfortably under the arm or along the curve of the back. The Kathleens, a shoulder bag, features a gentle curve that doesn’t compromise its interior capacity. Each design follows the same principle: structure without sacrificing comfort.

The decision to use Italian leather was inspired by Tell’s upbringing near horse farms in Pennsylvania, where leather was a familiar material, as well as her time studying in Florence. The hardware and jewelry were designed alongside the bags, echoing their forms and creating a cohesive finish. Offered in four colorways, Tell wanted to create a collection that felt both personal and universal—one designed to be worn across a range of genders and body types.

“In the campaign photos, there are different body types; there are different ages; there are different people,” Tell explains. “They get to decide if they want to wear that bag. It’s not me that says, ‘Okay, this is just for a woman.'”

Tell’s focus has remained squarely on design, down to the collection’s less visible details. To improve comfort and functionality, the “Les Tells” embossed tab on the Alexandras acts as a latch for the magnetic clasp that secures the flap. The Kathleens feature a hidden strap extender tucked inside the bag, plus a microfiber case for glasses. After two years of meticulous development, Tell believes the attention to detail has paid off. After all, you only get one chance to launch your first collection.

Tell has already started work on the next iteration, including a new set of the paper models that raised eyebrows when she began developing the debut collection. This time, however, she’s confident in the process that brought her designs to life. “I’ve had this gut feeling for so long that people are really going to appreciate the bags,” she says. “And I’m really ready to show the world.”

The Les Tells collection is now available at lestellsworld.com, with prices starting at $350.

An all star cast and leading director aren’t enough to distract eagle eyed critics.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The 2026 Christmas advert season is here, but with so many brands throwing their hat in the ring, standing out requires some real ingenuity. This year, brands have embraced a handcrafted approach, like Levi's stunning stop-motion film and Prada's Disney-inspired ad, but it seems there's an imposter among the ranks.

Dior's latest holiday ad has been lambasted for allegedly using AI-generated content, disguised as the work of human craft. While the best Christmas adverts don't follow a formula, Dior's 'animated' ad appears to be stolen valour, proving that audiences are hungry for authenticity when it comes to advertising.

Directed by French animator Sylvain Chomet, “Christian Dior’s Night in Color,” is (on the surface) a charming short film inspired by the founder's friendship with film star Marlene Dietrich. With an all-star cast including Robert Pattinson, Rihanna, Josh O’Connor and LaKeith Stanfield, the ad captures a familiar festive warmth, supported by its seemingly hand-illustrated animation.

watching "christian dior's night in color" and.... am i the only one noticing this is ai? https://t.co/7QEHjFtAMMNovember 4, 2026

But soon eagle-eyed viewers spotted some subtle discrepancies. Strange warped lettering, a lazy eye here, a misplaced phalange there – all signs seemed to point to AI slop.

But it was not the alleged AI that irked critics so much; it was the deception. A 'behind-the-scenes' look at production shows only sketches, and as the end credits roll, a short list of animators are listed with no mention of AI, (despite evidence suggesting otherwise). Paired with Chomet's creative legacy, Dior's intentions become clear – it wants you to believe this is 100% human animation.

Comments were suitably heated, with one X user writing "Your budget couldn't fit in an actual animation studio? You had to use AI? Disappointing." Another added "After the Prada commercial, its so easy to see its AI. Its so sad to see many good artists put their names behind this," while another added, "Oh Dior can rot in a ditch for not disclosing they resorted to ai-slop. All these 'pretty' visuals mean nothing if no artist animated them."

During this year's Christmas ad season, human-made craft has become synonymous with nostalgia and luxury – praiseworthy traits that earn brand's the audience seal of approval. Dior's sly attempt to capitalise on this trend has only peeved critics further, proving that even the most subtle AI blemishes will be called out. While Dior's AI augmentation is merely speculation, the brand's lack of transparency only aids in audience mistrust and frustration.

ICA Miami selects architect to renovate recently acquired building

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Rendering of ICA Miami’s new building Courtesy Gensler

The Institute of Contemporary Art, Miami (ICA Miami) has appointed the global architecture firm Gensler to renovate a 30,000-sq.-ft building the museum acquired in 2024, doubling its exhibition and public-programming space.

Steps away from its current venue in Miami’s Design District, the new wing is scheduled to open in late 2027—as the museum marks its tenth anniversary at its current location. Focused on expanding the free museum’s community outreach, the project will transform the ICA into a multi-site complex and house the first permanent display of its collection.

The museum’s new building formerly housed the De la Cruz Collection, a private museum that closed after the 2024 death of its co-founder Rosa de la Cruz. Since its $25m acquisition later that year, the site has mainly hosted the ICA’s educational programmes. It will close once construction begins. The renovation is part of a $40m capital campaign, supported entirely by private funds.

The new design will adapt the existing structure. “The building was developed to house a contemporary art collection,” Alex Gartenfeld, the ICA’s director and chief curator, tells The Art Newspaper. “But with the spaces we are constructing, we intend to enhance the museum’s story-driven narrative approach to the collection and exhibitions.”

Rendering of ICA Miami’s new building Courtesy Gensler

Eight new galleries will occupy the building’s first and second floors. Among them will be the museum’s first dedicated spaces for its growing permanent collection of more than 2,000 works.

“The programme between the two buildings is blended, but we will now have two full floors dedicated to our permanent collection,” Gartenfeld says. “Around every six months, we will refresh the collection display to reflect the collection’s emphasis and recent acquisitions.”

The entire third floor will be dedicated to public programming, including talks, symposia, lectures and performances. “Beyond adding galleries, this renovation will strengthen the ICA’s ability to support artists, expand learning and create a platform for exchange and belonging in one of the world’s most dynamic cultural cities,” says Bevin Savage Yamazaki, Gensler’s global culture and museums leader.

A reimagined façade with transparent components is key to the design. Gartenfeld says the façade aims to provide a connection between street-level traffic and the museum itself.

The renovations focus on visitor experience and accessibility as well. The lobby will be redesigned. And reconfigured outdoor areas will include social spaces, a sculpture deck and an expanded café. “We are aiming to create a more collective experience,” Gartenfeld says.

The museum expanded its exhibition space and reorganised its permanent collection for the first time in almost 100 years—with a special focus on Native American art

Several institutional construction projects have already opened this year, with more in the pipeline

Museum renovation showcases creative and sustainable design ideas to help transform contemporary life

Who Is Elena Kagan? Ideology, strategy, and the art of disagreement

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

On May 21, 2026, Justice Elena Kagan did something she had never done in nearly 16 years on the Supreme Court: she dissented alone in a signed decision. The case, Havana Docks v. Royal Caribbean Cruises, concerned whether cruise lines could face liability for using Cuban docks confiscated decades earlier. Eight justices, including Kagan’s liberal colleagues Sonia Sotomayor and Ketanji Brown Jackson, sided against the cruise lines. Kagan thought the majority had misunderstood a basic principle of property law: the interest originally confiscated had expired long before the cruise ships arrived.

The solitary dissent was unusual for a justice who has spent much of her tenure avoiding separate opinions. At a judicial conference in July 2025, Kagan observed that the court “speaks best when it speaks as a court,” and acknowledged that she sometimes drafts dissents only to decide against publishing them.

Kagan’s professional background is also unusual. She is the only current justice who arrived at the Supreme Court without previously serving as a judge. Before her appointment in 2010, she had been a law professor, dean of Harvard Law School, a White House adviser, and solicitor general of the United States. Her career was therefore shaped substantially by institutions in which persuasion, negotiation, and managing competing perspectives are routine.

But how much does this explain her jurisprudence? Ideological measures generally place Kagan to the right of her liberal colleagues, and her voting record contains meaningful differences from Sotomayor’s and Jackson’s. Yet those differences are far from uniform, and Kagan’s reluctance to write separately persists even when she reaches precisely the same result as her colleagues.

Understanding Kagan therefore requires examining not only how she votes, but how she chooses to agree and disagree.

Kagan has generally occupied a position to the right of the other liberal justices in measures of Supreme Court ideology. But the distance between them depends considerably on how ideology is measured, which cases are examined, and when the comparison is made.

The best-known measure is the Martin–Quinn score, which estimates the justices’ ideological positions from their voting patterns over time. In the 2024-25 term, Kagan’s score was −1.85, compared with −2.80 for Jackson and −4.19 for Sotomayor. Kagan was thus the rightmost of the three liberals, although she remained substantially to the left of the court’s median justice, Amy Coney Barrett, whose score was +0.53. The estimates for Jackson and Kagan had been closer in earlier terms (and, in fact, Jackson was initially positioned slightly to Kagan’s right).

The scores capture something revealing about how the justices vote, but they do not directly measure the substance of their legal reasoning or their willingness to compromise. Nor does a justice’s location relative to the other liberals necessarily reveal whether her own jurisprudence has changed. Martin–Quinn estimates are relative positions derived from voting coalitions, and those coalitions are themselves influenced by the court’s membership and docket.

The voting record offers a more concrete comparison. During the 2010–2021 terms, there were 56 contested decisions in which Kagan joined the majority while Sotomayor dissented, compared with just 19 in which their positions were reversed. Kagan was nearly three times as likely to be on the prevailing side when the two disagreed. That is a substantial difference, although it does not establish that every disagreement reflected an ideological division.

Justice Stephen Breyer, long considered a moderate liberal on the court, provides a useful point of reference. During their overlapping time on the court, Kagan joined the majority without Breyer 41 times, while Breyer joined the majority without Kagan 31 times. The more balanced relationship suggests that Kagan’s historical voting behavior often resembled Breyer’s more closely than Sotomayor’s.

The picture has changed since Jackson joined the court in 2022. Across 121 contested decisions in which Kagan and Sotomayor both participated through the 2025 term, they voted on the same side 109 times. Kagan prevailed without Sotomayor seven times, while Sotomayor prevailed without Kagan five times. Their historical difference in majority participation has thus narrowed dramatically.

The convergence is particularly visible in closely divided cases. Of 82 decisions decided 5–4 or 6–3 in which Kagan, Sotomayor, and Jackson all participated, the three liberals voted together in 71. When the court divided most sharply, Kagan usually found herself alongside her liberal colleagues rather than occupying a position between the liberal and conservative wings.

There are then two different stories in the numbers. Over much of her tenure, Kagan was more likely than Sotomayor to support outcomes favored by conservative justices. More recently, that distinction has diminished, particularly in closely divided cases. Whether this reflects a change in Kagan’s jurisprudence or the changing opportunities for cross-ideological agreement remains uncertain.

The cases in which she breaks with her liberal colleagues offer a way to examine that distinction more closely.

One way to identify a comparatively moderate justice is to examine how frequently she votes with colleagues across the ideological divide. By that measure, Kagan has demonstrated considerable independence from her liberal colleagues.

Across 26 terms, Kagan was the only liberal voting alongside conservative justices in 27 contested decisions, approximately 5.3% of her non-unanimous votes. Sotomayor occupied that position in 18 decisions, or 3.4%. Yet Breyer did so in 29 cases, representing 7.1% of his contested votes during the years he served alongside Kagan. When he was on the court, Breyer, rather than Kagan, was more likely to separate from the other liberals in that way.

The pattern becomes even more interesting with Jackson’s arrival. During the 2022–2025 terms, Kagan was the sole liberal voting with conservatives in nine contested cases, compared with ten for Jackson (though this frequency has decreased) and just two for Sotomayor. Jackson’s cross-ideological votes were evenly divided between joining majorities and dissents, while Kagan’s predominantly placed her in the majority. Thus, even when two justices cross ideological lines with similar frequency, they may not be doing the same thing.

These voting coalitions also raise a question about Kagan’s reputation as a consensus-builder. Does she actually attract more conservative support when writing for the court?

The analysis provides surprisingly little evidence that she does. Of Kagan’s 47 majority opinions in contested decisions, 27 attracted at least three conservative votes. Sotomayor achieved the same result in 30 of her 49 contested majority opinions. Their records are then similar, suggesting that Kagan’s reputation for building agreement is not necessarily reflected in a distinctive ability to assemble ideologically diverse majorities.

The individual cases also complicate the idea that Kagan’s departures from the liberal wing are simply strategic compromises. In 2020’s Kahler v. Kansas, for example, Kagan wrote for six justices, including five conservatives, holding that the Constitution does not require states to recognize an insanity defense based on a defendant’s inability to distinguish right from wrong. Breyer, joined by Ginsburg and Sotomayor, dissented. Kagan emphasized the historical diversity of insanity rules and the latitude afforded to state legislatures; Breyer saw Kansas’s approach as eliminating a fundamental protection deeply rooted in criminal law.

Four years later, Pulsifer v. United States presented a dispute over the meaning of a single conjunction in a federal sentencing statute. Kagan wrote for a six-justice majority, including five conservatives, adopting a reading that restricted defendants’ eligibility for relief from mandatory minimum sentences. Gorsuch dissented, joined by Sotomayor and Jackson, favoring an interpretation that would have made relief more widely available.

Neither case is easily explained as Kagan merely accommodating conservative colleagues and both show substantive disagreements over the meaning of legal rules, with significant consequences for criminal defendants.

These decisions also show why ideological distance and judicial method cannot be treated as interchangeable. Kagan’s cross-ideological votes sometimes reflect legal commitments that produce outcomes different from those favored by her liberal colleagues. They also suggest that her willingness to build agreement does not automatically lead her toward the narrowest available resolution.

If Kagan is not consistently more successful than Sotomayor at attracting conservative votes, the more distinctive feature of her jurisprudence may lie elsewhere: what she does after reaching agreement, and particularly when she decides that a disagreement warrants an opinion of her own.

The art of agreement: choosing when to write

The most distinctive feature of Kagan’s judicial behavior may have less to do with how she votes than with how she expresses agreement. During her 16 terms on the court, Kagan authored just 18 concurrences in signed decisions, compared with 81 for Sotomayor. Breyer, often described as an institutionally minded pragmatist, also wrote concurrences at more than twice Kagan’s rate during their overlapping service. Jackson has been considerably more prolific in her shorter tenure. Kagan’s comparative restraint is therefore striking even among justices who share many of her substantive positions.

The difference becomes even more revealing when the comparison is limited to cases in which the justices reached exactly the same outcome. Between 2010 and 2021, Kagan and Sotomayor both supported a conservative-authored majority opinion in 93 contested decisions. Sotomayor wrote separately in 13 of those cases; Kagan did so in just one. The difference is difficult to explain through ideology or case selection alone: the two justices were participating in the same decisions, agreeing with the same majorities, yet approaching the question of whether to write separately very differently.

The distinction has narrowed somewhat in recent years, but it has not vanished. During the 2022–2025 terms, Kagan wrote separately in three of 38 conservative-authored majorities she shared with Sotomayor, compared with five for Sotomayor. In 29 comparable cases involving Kagan and Jackson, Kagan wrote separately twice and Jackson eight times. These samples are smaller, but they reinforce the larger finding that Kagan is particularly reluctant to add her individual voice to an outcome she already supports.

Kagan herself has offered a remarkably candid explanation for this. Speaking at the 9th Circuit Judicial Conference in July 2025, she described the Supreme Court as an institution whose opinions should communicate a collective judgment. She acknowledged that producing a single institutional voice requires “a little bit of flexibility and compromise.” Separate opinions, she suggested, can complicate the law by presenting competing explanations of what the court has supposedly decided.

Yet Kagan’s restraint should not be mistaken for unqualified agreement. In 2024’s United States v. Rahimi, she joined Roberts’ majority upholding a federal restriction on firearm possession by individuals subject to certain domestic violence restraining orders. She also joined Sotomayor’s concurrence, which defended the majority’s application of New York State Rifle & Pistol Association Inc. v. Bruen while reiterating their disagreement with that earlier Second Amendment decision holding there to be a constitutional right to carry a firearm. Jackson wrote her own concurrence addressing the difficulties lower courts had encountered applying Bruen. All three liberals agreed with the outcome, but their choices about authorship differed.

A similar pattern appeared in 2023’s National Pork Producers Council v. Ross, in which the court rejected an argument by pork producers that a law violated the Constitution by regulating the pork industry outside of California. Kagan joined Sotomayor’s separate opinion explaining why they agreed with the judgment without embracing portions of Gorsuch’s reasoning that threatened to alter the established constitutional framework. Kagan was willing to register a qualification, but she did so by joining a colleague’s opinion rather than writing another.

These distinctions again complicate the portrayal of Kagan as a consensus-builder. The data do not show that she consistently attracts more conservative support to her own majority opinions than Sotomayor does. They show something different: Kagan is considerably more selective about articulating separate disagreements when she already supports the court’s disposition. That behavior may preserve a clearer institutional message, but it also leaves fewer public indication of her reservations about particular decisions.

The difference matters when interpreting Kagan’s ideological position. A justice who joins an opinion without writing separately may appear closer to its author than one who publishes a concurrence, even when both share the same substantive objection. Kagan’s record suggests the absence of a separate opinion can reflect a decision about judicial expression rather than any lack of disagreement.

The limits of agreement: textualism, precedent, and dissent

Kagan’s willingness to work across ideological lines has never meant avoiding deep disagreements with the court’s conservatives. Some of her sharpest opinions have challenged not only their conclusions but also their fidelity to the interpretive principles they profess to follow.

In a 2015 lecture at Harvard Law School honoring Antonin Scalia, Kagan famously remarked, “We’re all textualists now.” The observation acknowledged Scalia’s influence on statutory interpretation, including among justices who did not share his ideological commitments. Seven years later, in her dissent in West Virginia v. EPA, Kagan explicitly revisited that statement: “It seems I was wrong.”

Her objection concerned the majority’s use of the major questions doctrine to restrict the Environmental Protection Agency’s authority under the Clean Air Act. Kagan argued that the court had departed from ordinary statutory interpretation by demanding an unusually clear congressional authorization for agency action of substantial economic or political significance. In her account, the conservative majority’s claimed commitment to textualism yielded when the statutory text pointed toward an outcome it disfavored.

The exchange is revealing because Kagan was challenging the majority from within a methodology often associated with conservative jurisprudence. Her embrace of textualism did not entail agreement with the conservatives about what particular statutes meant, much less about how much authority courts should exercise over Congress and administrative agencies.

That distinction became even clearer in 2024’s Loper Bright Enterprises v. Raimondo. Writing for the three liberals, subject to Jackson’s recusal in one of the consolidated cases, Kagan defended Chevron deference (deferring to agency interpretations of ambiguous statutes) against the majority’s decision to overrule it. Her argument rested on statutory interpretation, congressional allocation of authority, administrative expertise, and the importance of precedent (a common theme for her). Textualism, in Kagan’s jurisprudence, does not necessarily require courts to resolve every statutory ambiguity without deference to an agency authorized to administer the law.

The same institutional concerns appear in her criticism of the court’s emergency docket. In Trump v. Wilcox, Kagan dissented from an order permitting the administration, pending further litigation, to remove officials from independent agencies. Joined by Sotomayor and Jackson, she objected both to the treatment of longstanding precedent and to the use of an expedited proceeding to alter the practical operation of existing law without ordinary briefing and argument.

These opinions provide an important counterweight to Kagan’s reputation for accommodation. Her restraint in writing separately does not preclude forceful disagreement when she believes the court has departed from established interpretive or institutional principles. Nor does that pattern necessarily distinguish her substantive positions from those of Sotomayor and Jackson, who joined these dissents.

The recurring question is therefore not simply whether Kagan is willing to compromise but where she draws the line between disagreements she can accommodate and those that she considers important enough to articulate publicly.

Sixteen years of decisions reveal a justice whose reputation for moderation captures only part of her judicial behavior. Kagan has historically been more willing than Sotomayor to join conservative-supported outcomes, and some of those differences reflect genuine disagreements about the law. Yet the gap has narrowed considerably in recent terms, particularly in cases that divide the court along ideological lines.

What distinguishes Kagan more consistently is her approach to disagreement. She is not uniquely successful at attracting conservative votes to her majority opinions. Rather, she is unusually reluctant to write separately when she can support an existing opinion, even in cases where her liberal colleagues reach the same result but feel compelled to explain their reservations. Her willingness to join another justice’s concurrence further complicates any assumption that silence necessarily signifies agreement.

The distinction has become more consequential as the court has moved rightward. Kagan’s opportunities to assemble majorities across ideological lines have diminished, while her dissents in disputes over administrative authority, precedent, and institutional power have become an increasingly public part of her jurisprudence.

Whether those patterns reflect a conscious strategy, enduring professional instincts, or some combination of the two cannot be determined from voting records alone. But the comparisons suggest that understanding Kagan requires looking beyond where she falls on an ideological scale. Her place on the court is defined both by the legal positions she takes and by the choices she makes about when – and how – to express them.

Dr. Adam Feldman is the creator and author of the blog Empirical SCOTUS and the Substack Legalytics.

We Tested the Viral ‘Slither Rig’ So You Don’t Have To

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 5, 2026 3:00 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

The hottest fishing news, tips, and tactics

When it comes to bass fishing baits, the Slither Rig is the latest craze on social media. Half flipping bait, half saltwater spoon, this setup certainly raises an eyebrow at first glance. But when an urchin is the most effective lure for catching a bass, who’s to say a saltwater spoon couldn’t work, too? So I set out to try this new contraption, and here’s what I found.

The Slither Rig consists of three parts: a saltwater jigging spoon, a flipping hook, and a flipping bait. Any saltwater jigging spoon will do; I picked up a pack of five for about 12 bucks on Amazon. You’ll want the spoon to weigh at least an ounce, because you’ll essentially be using it as a flipping weight. Remove the hooks from the spoon and replace the back one with a 4/0 flipping hook. Then slide your soft-plastic bait onto the hook, rig it weedless, and you’re ready to go.

The idea behind the Slither Rig is that you replace the traditional bullet-style weight with a spoon and use the weight of that spoon to plunge your bait through thick vegetation. For this style of fishing, you’ll need an extra-heavy/fast flipping/punching rod that’s at least 7 feet, 8 inches long. Pair that rod with a reel with a 7:1 or higher gear ratio, and spool the combo with 65-pound or stronger braid.

I fished the Slither Rig intermittently over the course of three trips, totaling six or seven hours with it. Toward the end of my testing, I finally got a couple of bites flipping thick gator grass and hyacinth mats. But up until that point, I found the Slither Rig to be more trouble than it was worth.

My biggest frustration with the Slither Rig was its inability to punch cleanly through the mat. Granted, I was only using a 1-ounce spoon in areas where I would typically use a 1 1/2-ounce tungsten weight. But this chunk of metal was already as long as my bait, so I didn’t want to go any bigger for fear of increasing the risk of a fish hitting the spoon and missing the bait carrying the hook. And I’m not sure it would have mattered anyway. The issue had less to do with the weight of the setup and more to do with the way it hinged in the middle.

Nine times out of ten, when I flipped the Slither Rig out, it would land flat on the cover and then bend in the middle, causing both the bait and the spoon to catch on the cover. It was like trying to push an already-sprung drywall anchor into a hole in the wall. The physics just didn’t work.

I found that I could flip the bait higher and get it to go through a little better, but the kerplunk of it smacking the mat was off-putting to me and no doubt disturbing to any bass sitting in the foot or two of water beneath the mats. So I was left with no option but to flip the bait onto the mat and then gently jiggle it until it fell through, which worked about a third of the time.

Personally, I don’t see a situation in which the Slither Rig would ever be a better choice than a traditional punching setup. Yes, the spoon adds flash that could have some appeal in stained water, perhaps. But silver tungsten weights can do the same thing, and they punch through cover far more cleanly.

While I did manage to get a couple of bites on the Slither Rig, I have zero doubt that I could have caught more fish had I flipped a traditional pegged punching rig or Texas rig through all of the same areas, simply because I would have been able to present my bait to the bass better.

Is the Slither Rig legit? Practically speaking, no. You may see someone on facespace talking about how it’s the next big thing, but don’t let them fool you. While I did prove to myself that I could get bit on it, the mechanics of the setup just aren’t conducive to heavy cover. Consider this one debunked.

Nintendo says it isn't planning any more Switch 2 price hikes

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Nintendo, which unveiled the results of the first half of its fiscal year 2027 earlier today, has now said it has access to enough quantities of memory to manufacture more Switch 2 consoles.

As a result, the platform holder does not plan to increase the price of the Switch 2. That's according to Reuters, citing Nintendo president Shuntaro Furukawa.

Nintendo, which has so far sold 26m Switch 2 consoles, has already raised the price of the console earlier this year. The price hikes, which took effect on 1st September in the US, Canada, Japan and Europe, saw the console's price increase by $50 / €30 / ¥10,000. Although that was technically the first price hike for the console itself, prior to the Switch 2's launch last year, Nintendo raised the price of Switch 2 accessories in the US as a result of the Trump Administration's tariffs.

The reason that would cause Nintendo to raise prices again is the same that already caused rival PlayStation and Xbox to do so: AI-driven, global memory and storage shortages.

PlayStation already raised PS5 and PS5 Pro prices, on several occasions. Xbox has likewise gone through multiple rounds of price hikes for its Series X and Series S consoles, with the most recent having taken place in August.

That same crisis pushed one of Nintendo's longtime ROM suppliers to exit the business, opting instead to focus on the more lucrative business segments. ROM memory is used in Switch cartridges, though Nintendo hasn't commented on the news.

Incidentally, Switch 2 hardware sales have been in decline for several quarters now compared to its banner launch in 2025. Still, Switch 2 sales are tracking ahead of the Switch 1.

Tool Trials: Putting Ridgid’s Cordless Crown Stapler To the Test

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Is a cordless stapler a real upgrade, or just a nice-to-have? I put one to the test to find out.

Our editors and experts handpick every product we feature. We may earn a commission from your purchases.Learn more.

Last spring, I was sitting at home with my family watching football on a Sunday when my wife caught me doing what I often do: scrolling Zillow. “What don’t you like about the house we’re in?” she asked. I told her that ever since I finished the basement, I’d missed having a shop space to work on projects.

Her answer: “Then build that garage you always wanted.”

Fast forward eight months, and my new garage is up. I’m insulating the main level and sheathing it with OSB before adding cabinets and shelving and moving on to the upstairs. It’s a big garage; we’re talking 30-plus rolls of insulation on the main level, so on a run to pick up the next half-dozen rolls, I wandered down the tool aisle and saw the Ridgid 18V Cordless 3/8-in. Crown Stapler staring back at me.

Beyond getting the garage insulation up faster, it looked like a tool I’d keep using on future wiring and upholstery projects, and the same goes for anyone re-screening a porch, recovering a chair seat, or tacking down low-voltage wire.

A cordless 3/8-in. crown stapler kit with a 2.0 Ah battery and charger, depth adjustment, an onboard wire and screen attachment, and a built-in LED light.

The Ridgid 18V Cordless 3/8-in. Crown Stapler is a 3-lb. battery-powered stapler that runs on Ridgid’s 18V battery platform. It features an integrated LED light, an onboard wire and screen attachment, a depth adjustment knob, and a belt hook.

It drives 3/8-in. crown (T50) staples from 1/4 to 9/16 in. long. Ridgid claims it can drive 105 staples per minute and up to 6,800 staples on a single charge of a 4.0 Ah battery.

One last note, and in full transparency, I’m a regular user of Ridgid tools myself: Ridgid offers a lifetime service agreement on its tools as long as you register them within 90 days of purchase.

My goal was to install a half-dozen rolls of insulation, then run wire to an outlet to try out the wire attachment. I wanted to see how easy the tool was to use, whether it jammed, and what I liked and didn’t like about it.

The short version: this tool is awesome. No jams, nothing wonky. It’s as simple as load, point, and pull the trigger. It took a little playing with the depth knob to get the staples seating at the right depth, but I had it figured out within a handful of staples.

I really like the wire attachment. I try to keep my wiring clean and neat, usually with zip ties and tie mounts. With this attachment, running the wire was simple, and the stapler did it much more neatly than I expected.

I was also pleasantly surprised by the onboard LED light. It has its own trigger, separate from the firing trigger, so I could turn it on only when I needed it.

It’s also easy on the battery. I put up six rolls of insulation during the test and a couple more off camera, and the indicator on the 2.0 Ah battery didn’t budge.

There were a couple of things I didn’t like, though they’re minor. First, Ridgid advertises dry-fire lockout on this stapler, which I took to mean it wouldn’t fire once the staples ran out. On mine, it still did. To be fair, it never marred the work surface, which Ridgid lists as one of the lockout’s benefits, so it may be doing part of its job. But it was annoying to pull the trigger and have nothing come out, and it made it easy to miss that I was out of staples.

Second, the depth drifted. After every 20 or 30 staples, I’d notice they were no longer seating at the depth I’d set and were starting to sit proud. I don’t know if that was due to denser 2x4s or inconsistencies between strips of staples, but it was something to keep an eye on. When I noticed it, I’d add a few clicks on the depth knob.

I really like this tool. If you’re only insulating a garage, it’s really nice to have, but not essential. You could get by with a standard stapler. That said, the LED light, the wire attachment, and the price, especially with the battery and charger included, make this a tool I know I’ll use more than I expected on future projects.

A cordless 3/8-in. crown stapler kit with a 2.0 Ah battery and charger, depth adjustment, an onboard wire and screen attachment, and a built-in LED light.

The tool alone retails at The Home Depot for $119, but for the last few months, there’s been a special buy that includes the tool, a charger, and a 2.0 Ah Ridgid battery for $104.

Ridgid is owned by Emerson Electric Co., which makes the red Ridgid plumbing and pipe tools directly. The orange Ridgid cordless power tools, including this stapler, are made by Techtronic Industries (TTI), the company behind Milwaukee and Ryobi, under a licensing agreement with Emerson.

No. Staple guns need staples that match the tool’s crown width, leg length, and wire gauge. The Ridgid 18V crown stapler takes 3/8-in. crown staples from 1/4 to 9/16 in. long, including standard T50 staples. Other staplers may use different types, like narrow-crown or other heavy-duty series, so check the tool or the manual before you buy.

Young Climbers Bag New Routes, Including First Ascent, in Rolwaling

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

An energetic foursome of young French climbers had a busy time recently in Nepal’s Rolwaling Valley, east of the Khumbu. Eric Bazon, Pierre Girot, Sophie Jacob, and Kilian Moni did two new routes, including a first ascent of a 5,205m rocky spire and one very difficult line. They then climbed another peak on a “rest” day.

As soon as the climbers reached Rolwaling, they had to change their primary goal, which was in poor condition. Their acclimatization was also progressing more slowly than expected. Instead, they focused their attention on a ridge they saw from their base camp every morning. On October 20, Bazon, Jacob, and Girot set off toward the summit, while Moni waited for them at the base.

“Before long, the climbing turned vertical, and the ridge became razor-sharp and exposed,” Bazon wrote. The climb took three days on the ridge, bivvies included, and led Bazon, Sophie Jacob, and Pierre Girot to the west summit of ChekiGo (6,128m). It took another full day and one more bivouac to reach base camp.

Cheki Go’s main summit was first climbed in 2005 by Bruce Normand of the UK and Oliver Von Rotz of Switzerland via the west face. A Spanish team led by Mikel Zabalza first reached the west summit via a direct route up the west face in 2017. The team had just returned from bagging the first ascent of Jugal II.

“It was quite an intense experience for a ‘secondary’ project: one that combined rock climbing on mediocre granite, improvised protection, and steep snow ridges, all while hauling our ropes on our backs,” said Bazon.

The ridge was technical enough to be a good start: 5c on rock and M4 on mixed terrain. In Alpine grades, the team assessed it as TD (very difficult).

Since the weather forecast looked good for another four days, the team decided to continue climbing. They split into two teams: Bazon and Girot headed for a peak previously climbed by elite American alpinists Domen Kastelic and Sam Hennessey in 2014: Chukyima Go (6,259m). Like their predecessors, the French team approached the west face from the Naa Valley in Rolwaling.

“There was this direct, narrow, and obvious couloir that simply begged to be climbed,” Bazon reported.

The climbers at an open bivy. Photo: Eric Bazon

The next morning, they found the west face in great condition, which allowed them to climb a direct route to the summit along rock cracks, steep ice, and varied mixed terrain.

It took them two days (October 27-28), with one cold bivy on the face with their feet in their backpacks, to complete the route’s 23 pitches. Their West Face Direct route is 900m long, and the team assessed it as ED (extremely difficult), with M6 on mixed sections, and an Alpine ice grade of AI5.

The photo below shows that the new French route, TchikitaMa Go, runs to the right of the previous line opened by the two Americans.

Left: the new route opened by the French team, compared, right, to the 2014 line by Domen Kastelic and Sam Hennessey. Photo: Eric Bazon/PlanetMountain

Meanwhile, Sophie Jacob and Kilian Moni went for an unclimbed peak that also rose right in front of their base camp: 5,205m Janjmu. The pair completed the first ascent on an aesthetic 600m line up the north face. They called the route The Eternal Stars (“Les etoiles eternelles”) after some friends who had recently perished.

“It felt just like being at home in the Alps, with the added challenge of high altitude,” the climbers reported. “We pushed ourselves to the limit, both physically and mentally, across demanding yet incredibly logical pitches.”

But this was still not enough for the pair. They squeezed in a final climb of Yalung Ri in what they called “a contemplative, active recovery” day. They then finished the trip by paragliding from the summit.

Kilian Moni flies from the top of Yalung Ri. Photo: Kilian Moni

Angela Benavides graduated university in journalism and specializes in high-altitude mountaineering and expedition news. She has been writing about climbing and mountaineering, adventure and outdoor sports for 20+ years.

Prior to that, Angela Benavides spent time at/worked at a number of local and international media. She is also experienced in outdoor-sport consultancy for sponsoring corporations, press manager and communication executive, and a published author.

ExplorersWeb is part of AllGear Digital's portfolio of media brands.

World of Warcraft: Forever's seemingly stellar launch continues as it soars to the top of Twitch's most viewed games, beating out the likes of League of Legends and Zelda

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

World of Warcraft: Forever launched last night after a hugely successful beta, and it's not just players getting in on the fun. Blizzard's MMO is also proving quite the attraction for spectators, soaring all the way up to the top of Twitch's most viewed games.

As of writing, World of Warcraft sits at around 385,000 viewers and streamer Ahmpy has the most popular WoW channel, pulling in around 18,300 live viewers.

For comparison, Twitch's ever-popular non-gaming Just Chatting category is the service's second most watched category with 145,000 viewers, and Riot Games' genre-leading MOBA League of Legends takes third place with 81,400 viewers.

Aion 2, another MMO released back in October, is sitting pretty at around 75,900 viewers, while Nintendo's newly released The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake (it's good!) has brought in a wonderful 63,100 watchers on the day of its arrival.

Perhaps contributing to World of Warcraft: Forever's success is the fact it launched with few problems, enjoying one of the smoothest day-one releases in the series' recent history. Its beta proved especially popular, with Blizzard developers calling it one of the most successful betas they've ever seen. One wonders how many of these current Twitch viewers are adults getting their WoW fix during work while their boss isn't looking?

Anyone who walks through the ER doors gets triaged, and I see what I can. This can be ear infections, severe dog bite wounds, or behavioral issues.

The most rewarding part of working in emergency veterinary medicine? When the patient is feeling good enough to try to bite you!

Initially, I wasnât sure if I wanted to go to veterinary school. I worked in a boarding clinic attached to a general practice that occasionally let me help on the medicine side, but I wasnât sold on vet med. I didnât get into veterinary school my first application round, so I found a job at a local emergency clinic. My working interview was so wild, I realized Iâd found my passion!

Emergencies come in waves! Some days are âDiarrhea Day,â where everything that comes in has diarrhea. I had âUrine Dayâ last week! By far, Iâd say GI upsets (vomiting, diarrhea, not eating) are the most common ER cases.

Seizures, protracted vomiting, severe weakness. I think anything that comes on suddenly should be investigated quickly!

If youâre worried, Iâm worried. You know your pet best, and if you feel something is âoffâ â donât wait! Even if itâs just for an exam or to get vitals, itâs well worth the peace of mind.

Itâs totally okay to be open about your budget and what you can and cannot do. The ER tends to be more expensive than a daytime practice, and we acknowledge that.  Our job is not to judge; itâs to help! If that means stabilizing until your vet can see you in a few hours, letâs do that.

I saw a lot of disinformation online. I figured if I was louder, I could drown out the bad advice.

I try to time my posts with real-world presentations. Heat stroke cases in the summer, toxicities around the holidays. Sometimes I just have a really interesting case, and I share it right then and there.

That veterinarians are humans too! I post a lot of information for other veterinary professionals, and I hope pet owners see the comments and realize how engaged the community is. Here is a group of people talking about vet med on their day off, learning about cases and new studies, and trying to keep up to date on the latest medicine.

Veterinary medicine is very science-focused. While we are always learning, thereâs a sort of structure to it that doesnât allow for much movement. My books are a whole different part of my brain, one where I can be creative and silly and make up worlds!

âAnother Damn Rescueâ is a spicy shifter romcom where a veterinarian finds an injured âdogâ on the side of the road, only to realize heâs a wolf shifter. Itâs ultimately a love letter to vet med, where I talk about the struggles we face, while also showing the humor and humanity. Oh, and how amazing veterinary technicians, assistants, and receptionists are!

Itâs more of a good time, kick your feet up and giggle sort of story! Pet owners might gain some insight into what itâs like to work in vet med in 2026. Veterinary professionals will enjoy seeing themselves accurately represented in media with a happy ever after!

The podcast is an extension of my social media â itâs a resource for other vets when theyâre not sure where to go. Itâs mostly focused on education for other veterinarians. I bring on specialists and let them pick a topic they feel passionate about. Think of it as continuing education.

Fatigue! Thereâs compassion and ethical fatigue, where we feel helpless in the face of obstacles outside our reach. Some days I think, âI didnât help anyone today.â And thatâs not true; itâs the story we tell ourselves when weâre in that headspace.

XYLITOL!! It can cause fatal low blood sugar and liver damage. Itâs in sugar-free gum, and lately theyâve been calling it âbirch sugar,â so not a lot of people realize itâs the same thing.

Because Women: Rethinking What Freedom of Movement Can Feel Like
