How Triathlon and Incurable Cancer Have Given Me a Way to Live Despite My Diagnosis

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Age-group triathlete Rebecca Jaussi shares her personal story with Triathlete of how receiving a cancer diagnosis drove her to signing up for and racing an Ironman 70.3.

I have spent most of my life making plans.

Endless lists of goals. Race calendars. Career milestones. Bucket-list adventures. I have always believed that if I worked hard enough and planned carefully enough, I might chart the course of my life.

Running and occasional triathlons are at the top of my goal-making lists.

I was a late starter professionally, choosing to stay home with my children when they were young. When I eventually returned to school, I didn’t ease my way back in. I was accepted into an accelerated doctoral nursing program, completing an intense year of coursework and clinicals before becoming a registered nurse, followed by three years of doctoral study. In 2020, I earned my doctorate in nursing practice and became a family nurse practitioner.

I have always been willing to push myself, physically and mentally, and my tenacity has benefited me.

I never imagined I would need it to fight cancer.

This story began with a fall. It was another beautiful morning on the North Shore of Oahu (where I was living at the time) in February 2023, and I was running a technical trail when I went down on a rocky section. I experienced a pop in my right hamstring and some pain. But I did what I always do and just kept going.

I finished the run. I experienced some pain afterward, but not enough, in my mind, to stop running for good. I took a few days off and then returned to the trails.

As a healthcare provider, I knew better. But as a runner, I convinced myself otherwise.

I continued running through an increasingly persistent, insidious pain. Eventually, my pace deteriorated from slow to a limping shuffle. Finally, I went to my colleague who practiced sports medicine, and was referred to physical therapy.

I started PT in May. My physical therapist told me I needed to stop running, and I thought that was an unreasonable course of action. There have been very few occasions in my life when I stopped running, including childbirth and surgery, and they were brief periods of time.

Running wasn’t simply exercise for me; it was where I found my flow state and truly part of my identity.

I didn’t feel ready or willing to give up running due to the pain but trusted my physical therapist, whom I considered a friend and a respected colleague, that this was the best course of action.

For three months, I walked on flat trails. I continued my physical therapy exercises and cross-trained but abstained from running or hiking.

When I returned for another assessment, my physical therapist was puzzled. I wasn’t improving. He suggested imaging, and that push changed everything.

I went in for an MRI of my right leg. The imaging was ordered for my mid-thigh, which was not where I had originally injured my hamstring. When I discussed this with the technician, I pointed out that it was in my hip region, so he modified the imaging order.

The MRI showed enlarged lymph nodes in my right groin, hip, and possibly behind my knee, which was an abnormal finding.

I had no recent infection or other obvious explanation for the swollen lymph nodes. I asked colleagues and peers for their opinions, and most believed this was a benign finding.

My mind vacillated between thinking that it was probably fine and the worst-case scenario of malignancy. It felt unsettling to me, and I decided to push for a biopsy rather than wait a few months for an ultrasound to see if the lymph nodes were still enlarged.

After the biopsy, I waited eight grueling days for the results. The physician, who was also my colleague and friend, told me the findings indicated that I had a form of B-cell non-Hodgkin lymphoma.

This was not easy to ingest, but I didn’t want to burden her with the heaviness of this diagnosis and began joking around. I was blasé and asked her, “How are we going to fix my leg so that I can run?” refusing to fall apart on the phone.

Then I hung up and broke down in front of my husband. Suddenly, all the plans I had spent my life making seemed irrelevant. Questions revolved in my head. What would happen to my family? My future? My career?

The next step I took was to do what I knew how to do, which was research my diagnosis to find out everything that I could about it. I wanted to know the prognosis, treatment options, statistics, anything to help me wrap my head around this.

What I discovered in this process was much more difficult to accept. There were things that I could not control.

I was driven to face the possibility that I may not live as long as I had expected. I had to accept that I would be living with the uncertainty of my health status and that fighting incurable cancer might become a permanent fixture in my life.

For someone who had devoted decades to building goals and marking off boxes, this was terrifying. There were no checklists for this.

I took time to process what I was facing and came to the recognition that I had a choice – not about living with cancer, but about living my life.

I was not going to allow this diagnosis to define me; I had a strong desire to live my life to the fullest with purpose and direction. I still wanted to work as a nurse practitioner for as long as I could. I still wanted to pursue medical humanitarian work. I still wanted to travel. I still wanted to be active.

In January, I was accepted into a clinical trial for immunotherapy.

Before treatment began, I did something that probably sounded slightly unreasonable to the people around me.

I signed up for Ironman 70.3 Hawaii on June 1, 2024.

I asked my oncologist and the lead investigator of my clinical trial whether I could participate. They told me that I was the first patient they knew of to ask about competing in a half Ironman while undergoing treatment.

They gave me their blessing with one caveat: It would be essential to listen to my body. This was going to be physically taxing on my body due to the cancer already exhausting my body and undergoing treatment in addition to the rigors of training for a 70.3.

I understood the risks. I also understood something else: Training gave me a goal and purpose to keep moving.

Although cancer had taken away my perception of control, it hadn’t taken away my ability to choose a direction.

I arrived in Kona on May 30, 2024. I was nervous and exhausted. I had received treatment the week before and was still recovering from it.

My younger brother, who had agreed to race with me when I asked, and I gathered our swag and attended the informational session. As we were walking out, I saw the iconic walls of all the participants, their bib numbers, and the “why” wall. Seeing my “why” story on the wall solidified my resolve in what I was doing.

The day before the race, I started receiving text messages with their pictures attached, early in the morning, from friends, family, and my whole care team from Fred Hutchinson Cancer Center, wearing a T-shirt that had my cancer ribbon with the words “Fight Cancer Like a Girl” on the front and “Team Rebecca 01” on the back. My amazing husband made nearly 100 shirts and sent them out, asking them to send me a picture and words of encouragement the day before the race. I felt so much love and support from the people in my life.

On June 1, 2024, I stood on the beach waiting for my wave to start at Ironman 70.3 Hawaii. I remember feeling incredibly grateful to be there and have the ability to push my body to the limits.

The race was hard; the winds were cranking so hard that several cyclists were blown into the lava rocks along the highway. It took some serious grit to keep going, but with my husband and kids driving by with my favorite songs blasting on the stereo, cheering me on, I was able to finish the bike.

By the time I started the run, it was midday, and the sun was searing, with temperatures climbing above 90 degrees. My body was fatigued, and every part of me wanted the race to be done. I dug deep. My mantra was “I can do hard things” on repeat. I kept moving.

During those final miles, rolling thoughts in my head surfaced: If I can fight cancer, I can finish this race.

There was an alteration in my thinking and in the purpose of that day; this was no longer about the race but rather an understanding that I was capable of moving forward.

I crossed the finish line with my brother beside me and my family waiting and cheering. I was overwhelmed by my emotions as I crossed the line.

My body was spent; however, there was something strangely beautiful about that exhaustion. I would rather be exhausted from swimming, cycling, and running than exhausted from lying still, consumed by the knowledge that I have incurable cancer.

My training looks different than it used to. Some days are hard, and my body needs downtime.

I’ve learned that listening to my body isn’t a weakness, or even optional at times, but rather a way for me to keep up the fight.

With every step I take, every mile I cycle, every lap that I swim, it is more than just training; it serves as a reminder that my body is still mine and truly a gift to be cherished.

My cancer diagnosis has altered my relationship with time. I functioned at a faster pace and was always thinking ahead about what was next: the next race, the next degree, the next goal, the next item to add to my list.

I am more focused on the mile that I am in right now. I have not stopped making plans; I just hold them differently.

My desires to work, travel, race, and serve others remain, but their priority and intensity are more flexible, as my health and energy can be fluid.

This diagnosis has shifted my perspective – I no longer believe that my worth or value depends on completing every goal I write down.

Some days a win is simply to get out the door. Sometimes it is a slow swim, and the distance may be shorter than planned. Sometimes it is walking instead of running. Sometimes it is resting.

And sometimes, it is standing at a finish line I wasn’t sure I would ever reach.

The trajectory of my life feels unsure and more constrained. However, I know that running, swimming, and cycling are things I can do at the moment, and they make me feel alive.

Honestly, for me, that’s what endurance sport has become. This is not a means of escape from cancer but rather my way of living despite it.

One step, one mile, one breath, I will keep going.

Claude’s new Personal Finance tool aims to help analyze your spending – read-only, of course

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Anthropic has released what appears to be its answer to ChatGPT Finances.

You can now share your financial information with Claude and ask the chatbot for insights on your spending.

Also: Researchers asked people to use AI chatbots for election info

Personal Finance in Claude, which Anthropic announced Thursday, reviews your connected accounts through read-only access via Plaid. That security measure follows OpenAI’s equivalent feature, ChatGPT Finances, which launched in May. Here’s what to know.

Functionally, this feature appears similar to ChatGPT Finances: It lets you link as many bank and credit card accounts as you’d like to Claude via Plaid with read-only access. You can then ask Claude where you spent your money this month or whether you can afford to take a vacation in six months, for example.

(Disclosure: Ziff Davis, ZDNET’s parent company, filed an April 2025 lawsuit against OpenAI, alleging it infringed Ziff Davis copyrights in training and operating its AI systems.)

Also: Stop sharing your passwords with AI agents

According to Anthropic, the feature is a response to existing use patterns: the company’s April usage report found that financial advice was one of the main topics users ask Claude for help with.

Given the privacy concerns around sharing sensitive data with AI systems, especially agents, Anthropic emphasized which data the new feature will access and how.

“For the accounts you choose to connect (and only those), Claude sees the bank’s name, each account’s name, type, and last four digits, balances and credit limits, and up to two years of transactions. When you chat with Claude in the Personal Finance hub, those conversations aren’t used to train our models.”

That last part is important: Personal Finance in Claude is its own mode with its own protections. If you share or discuss your financial data with Claude outside that “hub,” it’s subject only to your Claude privacy settings.

The idea behind the feature is that you can ask follow-up questions and request more insights about spending habits, trends over time, and future goals based on what Claude finds, similar to any other Claude conversation. The tool will also generate tables and charts to visualize your financial information.

“You can ask for a weekly spending chart, a calendar of what’s due, or a dashboard of the numbers you choose, and Claude builds it as an artifact you can keep adjusting and coming back to,” an Anthropic representative told ZDNET via email.

Also: I connected ChatGPT to my bank, and it’s my go-to finance app now

You can also expect an ad-free experience in the feature.

“Like the rest of Claude, Personal Finance has no ads or sponsored recommendations, and it doesn’t offer credit cards, loans, or savings accounts of its own, so your money questions get a straight answer,” the company said in the announcement.

The company added that because Claude is using actual transaction data and not memory, the feature shouldn’t hallucinate.

“It can list the exact transactions behind any number so you can check its math,” Anthropic said via email. “Like any AI, it can make mistakes, so for anything important, we recommend confirming with your bank.”

Still, the difference — and for many, the appeal — here is the natural language interface. Other finance apps don’t necessarily have an easy, accessible chat function where users can shoot off quick questions like they would to a human financial planner. These assistants also present historical budget analysis in plain English, rather than requiring users to interpret dashboards themselves, which users may see as a perk.

Also: 5 ways to use AI when your budget is tight

On Reddit, many users find ChatGPT Finances to be accurate and reliable. One Reddit user said ChatGPT Finance seemed to have Mint or Monarch potential, but noted it was still “weaker” than those apps in some areas, such as historical syncing, high-quality dashboards, and generating full statement PDFs.

ZDNET editor-in-chief Elyse Betters Picaro was “incredibly impressed” with ChatGPT Finances when she tried it out, and still uses it as one of several financial tools (not a replacement for professional advice). She also has her privacy settings in ChatGPT structured to reduce any security risks.

“ChatGPT has genuinely helped me make better financial decisions,” she wrote.

Anthropic noted that users shouldn’t look to Personal Finance for Claude as a total authority. “Personal Finance is a first step. For life’s bigger money decisions, we think people are best served by Claude working alongside the licensed professionals and regulated firms they trust,” the company said in the announcement.

“We’re exploring how to make that possible in Claude, and we’d like to hear from banks, wealth managers, and financial services companies who want to help shape it” — indicating a more integrated financial planning system within Claude may be on the way.

South Korea Pushes 22% Crypto Tax to 2029 in Fourth Delay, Offers Three-Year Loss Carryforward

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

South Korea's government will ask lawmakers to delay its 22% crypto gains tax to 2029 and let investors carry losses forward for up to three years.

South Korea’s government plans to push back its tax on cryptocurrency gains by another two years to 2029, Finance and Economy Minister Lee Hyeong-il said Thursday, in what would be the fourth delay since the levy was written into law.

The tax is scheduled to take effect in January. To postpone it, the National Assembly must amend the Income Tax Act before then, and Lee said the government would seek lawmakers’ approval during this year’s regular session.

“It weighs heavily on me to propose another delay after the tax has already been postponed three times,” Lee said at a press briefing in Seoul.

Korea legislated the tax in 2020 for a 2022 start. It was then pushed to 2023, 2025 and 2027 as officials conceded that tax systems and investor protections were not ready.

The main reason for the new delay is data on overseas trading. When investors move tokens bought abroad onto a Korean exchange, the exchange may have no record of the purchase price needed to calculate a taxable gain.

That gap closes in 2029, when Korea and the United States begin exchanging information under the OECD-backed Crypto-Asset Reporting Framework, or CARF. Introducing the tax before then could put Korea’s domestic crypto market at a disadvantage, the government said.

“Once we begin receiving cryptocurrency transaction data from the United States in 2029, we will have enough information to tax investors who use US exchanges on the same basis as those who trade in the domestic market,” Lee said.

The government also cited the need to align the tax with the proposed Digital Asset Act, which would set rules for digital asset businesses and strengthen investor protection. Korea’s broader crypto rulebook has been moving on several fronts, including a Bank of Korea push to write stablecoins into foreign exchange law.

Exchanges, investors and lawmakers had pressed for more time, with exchanges saying unclear reporting requirements held up system development and testing.

The rate itself is not changing. Individuals would pay 22%, including local income tax, on annual net income above 2.5 million won, or about $1,869, from selling or lending virtual assets such as bitcoin.

What is new is a proposed three-year loss carryforward. Under current law, only losses from the same year can offset gains, so an investor could owe tax in a profitable year even after losing more the year before.

Under the proposal, losses realized in 2029 could offset gains through 2032. Losses realized before the tax takes effect would not be eligible, the government said, citing the volatility of crypto prices as the reason for the change.

The carryforward would follow similar practices in the U.S. and Britain, the finance ministry said. It also said it will work with the Financial Services Commission to speed up the digital asset law.

The government plans to use the extra two years to collect overseas trading records and analyze them alongside domestic tax data. A working group of industry representatives, experts and government officials will review tax rules for different types of transactions and ways to simplify filing through the first half of 2027.

If the review finds that more legislation is needed, the government plans to submit amendments during the National Assembly’s 2027 regular session. Detailed tax guidelines are due in the first half of 2028.

For Korea’s large base of retail crypto traders, the announcement extends a tax-free window that was supposed to close in 2022. Whether 2029 holds depends on the Assembly passing the amendment in the coming weeks and on the U.S. data exchange starting on schedule.

Bull Bitcoin Becomes First Exchange to Add Payjoin Privacy Deposits, Withdrawals, and Payjoin-by-Default Trading

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Bull Bitcoin announced deep Payjoin integration with its exchange, claiming to be the first Bitcoin exchange in the world to offer Payjoin as a deposit and withdrawal option. The update unlocks two new concepts: “Payjoin-by-default and Payjoin trading,” according to he press release shared with Bitcoin Magazine.

Founded by Francis Pouliot in Canada in 2013, Bull Bitcoin is the world’s longest-running Bitcoin-only exchange. Its non-custodial platform lets customers in Canada, Europe, and Latin America buy Bitcoin directly into their own wallet and sell Bitcoin to their bank account. Bull Bitcoin also develops BULL Wallet, a free and open-source self-custodial Bitcoin wallet. The company has been self-funded since its inception.

Payjoin is a Bitcoin onchain privacy protocol that breaks basic assumptions made by on-chain analysis firms about user behaviour. One such assumption is that one of the unspect transaction outputs (UTXOs) resulting from a bitcoin transaction will be change returning to the sender. Or that amounts are more often than not denominated in dollars. Payjoin protocols add an off-chain coordination layer such that the amounts sent and returned to the sender obfuscate user intent and thus protect their privacy on-chain. Payjoin does not require a hard fork or a soft fork into Bitcoin consensus. Instead, it must be adopted by user wallets and exchanges to scale, but it also does not depend on collective action to benefit individual users. Bull Bitcoin’s Payjoin integration is closely based on the Payjoin Foundation‘s work.

Every time a BULL Wallet user buys or sells Bitcoin on-chain via their built-in exchange, that transaction will be a Payjoin by default. This will allow Bitcoin buyers to passively aggregate their wallet’s UTXOs, which can reduce their Bitcoin mining fees in the future. “Many Bitcoin users do not even know that they must periodically “merge” their coins by spending them to a new address in their Bitcoin wallet, if they want to reduce the economic costs of future Bitcoin transactions,” explained the press release. Payjoin-by-default automates this process. Not only does this help reduce the size of Bitcoin’s UTXO set, making it easier for everyone to run a Bitcoin node, but it also reduces fee pressure for every Bitcoin user.

Payjoin trading is a novel concept that protects users from malicious actors trying to gain insights into their financial habits by conducting chain analysis. Because transaction amounts in Bitcoin are public, it’s possible to reverse-engineer information such as someone’s salary or trading strategies such as dollar-cost averaging. Bull Bitcoin is a non-custodial exchange, so every trade leaves an on-chain trail.

The widespread adoption of Payjoin in the Bitcoin network makes common input ownership and change output detection heuristics less reliable. Bull Bitcoin’s Payjoin implementation reduces round numbers in the amounts sent as well as address reuse, and reduces the possibility of third-party onlookers guessing the fiat value of Bitcoin transactions. Introducing Payjoin-by-default and Payjoin trading means that the more people use Bull Bitcoin, the more privacy the Bitcoin network gains as a whole; “This is part of our philosophy to align the incentives of our company with the interests of all Bitcoin users.”

In December 2024, BULL Wallet became the world’s first Bitcoin wallet to integrate the Payjoin V2 protocol. This was a highly experimental project coordinated with Dan Gould and the Payjoin Dev Kit team, who have since founded the Payjoin Foundation.

Researchers have used the insights from this experimentation to improve the Payjoin experience to make it more seamless and resistant to attacks. The software powering Payjoin, both for the wallet and for the exchange, is fully free and open-source.

To benefit from these features, all a user has to do is download BULL Wallet, create a Bull Bitcoin exchange account, and log into their account via the BULL mobile app; “The rest happens automatically, like magic!”

Paul Hamlyn Foundation announces recipients of £75,000 Awards for Artists

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Award winners left to right: Hayley Tompkins, Daudi Matsiko, Bushra El-Turk, Linda Bell, Olivia Chaney, Rory Pilgrim, Ayanna Witter-Johnson, Georgina Starr, Charlotte Keeffe, (Racheal Crowther not present).Photo credit: Emile Holba

The Turner prize nominee Rory Pilgrim and the Venice Biennale artist Hayley Tompkins are among the recipients of this year’s Paul Hamlyn Foundation’s Awards for Artists. The £75,000 awards were granted to five artists and five composers.

In the wake of the ongoing cost of living crisis, the awards are timely. “Unique in its no-strings-attached funding model, the [Hamlyn] awards for artists allows recipients to use their award as they wish, providing them the freedom to develop their creative ideas, alleviating the pressures they may be facing,” says a press statement.

Tompkins was part of a trio of artists, alongside Corin Sworn and Duncan Campbell, who showed works in the Scotland + Venice exhibition in 2013. Her installations combine painting with a variety of other media, including found objects, digital films, photography and furniture. “It still hasn’t felt real yet, but I do know with more resources there will be room for me to feel and move a bit differently now,” she says in a statement.

Pilgrim’s work explores how “activism, spirituality and music can create spaces for connection”, adds the statement. Bristol-born Pilgrim was nominated for the Turner Prize in 2023 for their commission RAFTS at Serpentine Galleries and Barking Town Hall. “After working on many intensive processes, [the award] gives invaluable breathing space, autonomy and financial security to find joy and excitement in creativity,” Pilgrim says.

The other successful artists this year are Georgina Starr, who is known for performance works such as Androgynous Egg (2017), Dublin-born artist Racheal Crowther and the learning disabled artist Linda Bell. Judges include Joe Hill, the director of Yorkshire Sculpture Park, Melissa Blanchflower, a senior curator at Turner Contemporary in Margate, and Sheryll Catto, the artistic director of ActionSpace.

Since 1994, Paul Hamlyn Foundation has made 377 awards to artists with over £12m in funding; Phyllida Barlow, Yinka Shonibare and Alberta Whittle are among the previous recipients. Five composers including Daudi Matsiko and Ayanna Witter-Johnson were also recognised.

Got Fungus Gnats? This Easy Potato Trick Lures Hidden Larvae Out of Houseplant Soil

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Beautiful plants and ideas to brighten your garden.

Expert tips for growing fruit, vegetables and herbs at home.

Sign up to get fresh gardening ideas, inspiration and expert advice delivered straight to your inbox.

7 Kitchen Scraps You Should Save for Backyard Birds This Fall – Give Them a Helping Hand as Temperatures Drop

7 Household Trash Items You Should Save in Fall to Protect Plants From Frost

Why You Should Never Ignore a Spider Plant With Brown Leaf Tips in Fall

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Those tiny black flies are only part of the story. A raw potato slice on the soil can reveal what's underneath for next to nothing.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

You water your houseplant, and a little cloud of tiny black flies lifts off the soil. A minute later, they're on the window. Fungus gnats turn up in many homes once fall arrives, and they can hang around all winter. The next batch is already growing in the pot, and a fungus gnat potato trap is an easy way to find them.

Before you read up on how to get rid of fungus gnats, take a look at what's going on in the pot. The adult flies you keep swatting don't feed on your plants, but their larvae are a different story. Those live in the damp potting mix just under the surface, out of sight unless you go digging.

One raw potato saves you the digging. Lay a slice on the soil, and the larvae come up to feed on it. Lift it a couple of days later, and whatever is in the pot is often feeding on the underside. Odds are there's a potato in the pantry already, so finding out costs you pretty much nothing, really.

A fungus gnat's whole life happens in or around the pot. They're small flies, often in the genus Bradysia, and a female can lay up to 200 eggs in damp potting mix. Once they hatch, the larvae eat for a couple of weeks and pupate near the surface, and new adults show up a week or so later. At room temperature, that's roughly three to four weeks per generation – so a few flies in October can be a lot more by the holidays.

It's the larvae doing the eating. Their usual food is fungi and rotting organic matter, but they'll go after fine root hairs too. While a minor infestation won't usually do any harm, enough of them can damage roots and stunt growth, especially in seedlings and young plants. Watch for something like a tiny worm: clear or whitish, no legs, a shiny black head, ¼ inch (6 mm) long at most.

Cut a raw potato into slices about ¼ inch (6.4 mm) thick. Set one cut side down on the potting mix, and press it lightly so it sits flat. That contact is the whole trick. Larvae move up to the potato as a food source and gather on the moist underside where it touches the soil.

Give it a day or two before the first look. Flip the slice and check its underside, then the soil where it sat, for tiny clear larvae. Against dark, wet potting mix, they're hard to pick out, though, so a magnifying glass, like this Rongon 10X folding pocket magnifier from Amazon, helps a lot. Nothing there yet? Set it back down for another day or two. Sometimes larvae can take a few days to find the potato.

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Don't leave it there for good, though. Raw potato breaks down in damp soil, and a rotting slice is just more of the decaying material larvae feed on. Toss the old one in the trash, larvae and all, and put down a fresh slice. Used like this, potato slices for fungus gnats let you track whether the numbers are dropping. Eggs won't show up on it. They don't feed, so they stay put in the mix.

So, do potatoes kill fungus gnats? Not really. The potato doesn't poison anything – it just draws feeding larvae up from the roots, and tossing a slice with larvae on it removes those ones. Eggs, pupae, and the adults flying around the room aren't affected.

Use the potato to check on things, then reach for something else to fix it. Say the slice comes up clean. That's nice, but it doesn't mean the pot's clear. Maybe there are only a few larvae, or they weren't feeding much. If flies keep hanging around, put down another slice.

Too much water keeps a lot of fungus gnat problems going. Short days and cool rooms slow houseplants down in fall and winter, so they use less water. Water like it's still July, and the mix stays wet, which eggs and larvae love. Let the top inch (2.5cm) dry before watering again. Empty the saucer, and make sure the pot drains. Old mix holds extra water as it breaks down. If a plant's due for repotting, look for a fresh mix with perlite for drainage, like the Back to the Roots Indoor Potting Mix, available from Walmart.

Yellow sticky traps are for the adults. Push a few into the pot – you can buy sticky traps cheaply from Amazon. Each female stuck to one won't lay any more eggs, and the count tells you if things are improving. Down in the soil, the larvae aren't touched. If the gnats won't let up, try Bti (Bacillus thuringiensis subsp. israelensis), a naturally occurring soil bacterium. Despite the name, it isn't the Bt sprayed on caterpillars. This strain targets only fly larvae.

Summit Mosquito Bits use Bti to control both mosquito and fungus gnat larvae. Rather than scattering the granules on the soil, it's best to soak them in water for about 30 minutes, then apply that water to your houseplant soil. Repeat application about every five days, since Bti doesn't last long indoors.

Beneficial nematodes (Steinernema feltiae), such as BioLogic's Scanmask nematodes, are another organic option. These microscopic roundworms seek out fungus gnat larvae in potting soil. They enter the larvae and release bacteria that kill them, then feed and reproduce inside their bodies. It's pretty gruesome, but effective. They arrive alive in the mail, so use them right away. Keep the soil moist and the temperature between 60 and 90°Fahrenheit (16–32°C), or they won't work. Whichever you buy, check that the label allows indoor potted plants, then follow it.

Catching larvae early makes a fungus gnat problem much easier to control. A few larvae in one pot make for a far smaller job than a houseful of flies a month from now – and a potato slice is a cheap way to see whether your houseplant soil is hiding them.

So keep a potato around this winter. Set a fresh slice down whenever the flies show up again, and go easier on the watering while days are short. Fungus gnats have a much harder time of it once the top of the soil dries out between drinks!

Tyler’s passion began with indoor gardening and deepened as he studied plant-fungi interactions in controlled settings. With a microbiology background focused on fungi, he’s spent over a decade solving tough and intricate gardening problems. After spinal injuries and brain surgery, Tyler’s approach to gardening changed. It became less about the hobby and more about recovery and adapting to physical limits. His growing success shows that disability doesn’t have to stop you from your goals.

Mass Effect petition has enough signatures for the Canadian Government to potentially save BioWare

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Following news of EA's decision to shut down iconic RPG studio BioWare, Canadian fans of the studio have escalated their grievances to the governmental level, calling on the Canadian Government to step in and save the Canadian studio from closure, seemingly after Canada's New Democratic Party leader Avi Lewis called on the government to do so.

Fans have submitted a petition to the Canadian House of Commons arguing for the government to find a way for BioWare to continue, such as through funding that allows the studio to buy the Mass Effect and other IPs and go independent.

The petition, number e-7898, argues that "BioWare is one of Canada's most important cultural exports" and that the studio's closure by its American parent company "represents an immense loss to and possibly a targeted attack on Canada's cultural legacy."

"BioWare's productions champion the values of inclusivity, tolerance, democracy, justice, and civil rights that are at the centre of Canada's national identity," it continued.

The petition opened for signatures on 4th of November, and has already amassed 1,700 signatures. By law, petitions that surpass 500 valid signatures from Canadian citizens or residents are entered into the House of Commons' agenda, meaning they will be presented in front of the parliament.

As encouraging as that may be, it's worth keeping in mind that it may not result in meaningful change. Even if the Canadian Government agrees with the petition's goals, it cannot force EA to sell BioWare or its various IPs.

EA notably didn't shop the studio around before closing it down, and the publisher has a history of closing down owned studios rather than selling them off. EA has also kept the IPs of its various shuttered studios.

The community's reaction to BioWare's closure has taken different forms. Hundreds of thousands signed a different petition to save Mass Effect. The people behind the movement have purchased billboards in Times Square in New York City to advertise it, with plans for more outside The Game Awards ceremony in December.

Meanwhile, former BioWare developers have spoken about the last days of BioWare, and the work they've poured into Mass Effect Universe, the studio's final project before it was shuttered.

One museum visit a month could improve health, new report finds

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The author Daisy Fancourt and Jenny Waldman, the Art Fund's director, view the ‘Recommended Dose of Art: A Prescription for Health and Wellbeing’ report at London’s Wallace Collection

David Parry/Assignments/Press Association

Visiting a museum or art gallery for just one hour a month could significantly improve people's physical and mental health, according to a new report which calls for cultural engagement to become as routine as exercise and healthy eating.

The research, commissioned by the charity Art Fund in partnership with University College London professor Daisy Fancourt, recommends that people spend at least 60 minutes with art in museums and galleries every month.

However, the survey of 2,000 UK adults found that 89% fail to meet this proposed target, equivalent to an estimated 49.5 million people.

The report, “The Recommended Dose of Art: A Prescription for Health and Wellbeing”, draws together scientific evidence suggesting that regular cultural engagement can reduce stress, improve cognitive health and potentially extend life expectancy.

Previous studies have shown that regular museum and gallery visits could nearly halve the risk of developing depression over the following decade. Other studies have linked cultural participation with reduced dementia risk and lower levels of loneliness.

Viewing original artworks in galleries was also associated with a 22% greater reduction in the stress hormone cortisol compared with viewing reproductions.

Fancourt, whose book Art Cure on the scientific benefits of art came out earlier this year, says regular engagement with art should be considered a health-promoting behaviour alongside exercise, a balanced diet and spending time outdoors.

“Guidelines like eating our 5-a-day of fruit and vegetables and getting our 10,000 steps have proven effective in helping us to prioritise other health-promoting behaviours in our busy lives,” she says in a statement. “Adding regular arts engagement to this list could be a simple and joyful way of investing further in our health and wellbeing.”

But the findings also raise questions about whether Britain's struggling museums have the resources to encourage more frequent visits. Jenny Waldman, the director of Art Fund, tells The Art Newspaper that the research strengthens the case for sustained public investment in museums, including protecting free admission at national institutions and supporting local and regional collections.

“We want to see sustained, long-term public investment across the museum sector, so that all museums have the resources to care for their collections and welcome the widest possible audiences,” she says.

Waldman acknowledged that removing admission charges alone would not overcome barriers to visiting museums, including accessibility and the resources available to institutions.

She says Art Fund's research among museum directors found that 36% of museums had reduced their opening hours because of insufficient funding and staffing.

“If we want museums to play a greater role in supporting public health, we must invest in the sector and give them the means to do so,” she adds.

Waldman also points to international developments, including Greece's introduction of arts prescribing into its national healthcare system and growing recognition of cultural organisations as public health assets. She also notes efforts closer to home: “Scotland's Chief Medical Officer, Sir Gregor Smith, recently published a report recognising arts organisations as important public health assets and calling for arts and culture to be embedded more widely in Scotland's approach to public health.”

The approximately $9m project included modernising 20,000 sq. ft of galleries and bringing key objects out of storage for public display for the first time

The museum has been praised for the ambitious way it has shared its collections and for its social outreach

After months without income due to coronavirus closures, new survey shows that museums are operating with just 25% of previous visitor numbers

From the future of the Arts Council to AI, there are several complicated issues for the incoming culture secretary to tackle

3 ways Bluetooth 7 upgrades your devices beyond lossless audio

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Bluetooth 7 could make your smart home devices easier to set up, for one. Here's what we know about the new core specification.

Bluetooth 7 is here, and each new specification from the Bluetooth Special Interest Group (SIG) offers a glimpse of what we can expect from our devices in the future. The major feature incorporated into the new core standard is High Data Throughput (HDT), which can enable lossless wireless audio and improved multi-channel spatial audio on more Bluetooth devices.

Also: Apple has a Bluetooth problem, and Android is taking advantage

Bluetooth 7’s standout feature is that it can achieve wireless lossless audio. However, the improvements to the Bluetooth stack architecture — particularly, expanding the Bluetooth spectrum — allow for faster data transfer, improved energy efficiency, more resilient connections, and create more room for devices and their data-intensive applications.

HDT means good news for audiophiles, smart home enthusiasts, VR/XR fans, wellness wearable aficionados, AI companion devotees, and much more. I explained applications of HDT in a wireless audio context earlier this year. Now that it’s officially adopted, here’s what you can expect.

A major milestone in Bluetooth 7 is the ability to stream lossless audio over a wireless connection. Bluetooth is lossy, requiring data compression that diminishes audio quality. HDT’s faster data transfer and greater wireless data capacity enable it to handle the massive volume of data required to achieve lossless quality.

Also: Your Bose headphones are getting a major Bluetooth upgrade – what to expect

Audiophiles typically avoid wireless headphones for intensive listening due to the necessary data compression, opting for wired headphones that can accommodate larger lossless files. Bluetooth’s innovation here is widely regarded as a major leap in wireless technology.

HDT also promises to standardize spatial audio, with expanded data transfers allowing for multi-channel audio setups. Standardizing this form of audio transmission could mean consumers can expect more affordable brands and products to adopt this premium feature.

Every consumer-friendly feature possible with Bluetooth 7 can be traced back to its ability to accommodate more devices and their ever-expanding data needs. According to a Bluetooth blog post, Bluetooth HDT delivers nearly four times the previous maximum Bluetooth throughput.

Data throughput is the amount of data received, transmitted, and processed over the Bluetooth network. With nearly four times the processing power, HDT can move more data, faster. HDT increases Bluetooth’s maximum supported bitrate to 7.5 Mbps, up from the current 2 Mbps.

Also: Beware of Bluetooth sniffers: How your favorite gadgets could be exposing your data

The result is shorter download times for a device’s firmware update, faster device location when Find My Device is activated, or faster transfer of a large photo album via Android’s QuickShare or Apple’s AirDrop.

In the same blog post, Øyvind Strøm, executive vice president of the short-range business unit at Nordic Semiconductor, gives examples of a smartwatch syncing hours’ worth of workout data to your phone in seconds, or a pair of headphones, a smart speaker, or a security camera that can download a firmware update with nearly zero interruption to the user experience.

In a smart home context, Bluetooth Low Energy (LE) is the wireless technology that works in the background, connecting your devices. Bluetooth LE is the baseline technology for quick device discovery and connection. The most recent example of this is Apple’s HomeView, which uses Bluetooth LE to discover a nearby, trusted iPhone to unlock that user’s personalized home hub display.

In a smart home, Bluetooth LE works in the background; the less you feel its presence through lags, wait times, and dropouts, the better it’s working. HDT’s goal is to make Bluetooth connectivity frictionless, which is especially valuable when your smart lights, shades, speakers, TVs, refrigerators, and more rely on the technology.

Also: Forget Wi-Fi: Apple is betting on Bluetooth with its new home devices

For example, HDT’s improved data throughput could allow doorbells, cameras, and robot vacuums to quickly offload large amounts of data to your smart home hub. It could also enable near-instantaneous setup of a new device onto your smart home network and firmware updates.

Finally, HDT also addressed network stability and reliability, especially in high-traffic areas such as apartment complexes and dense cities, where a busy network can cause delays and backups. Alongside improvements to Wi-Fi and Thread, a truly seamless smart home is on the horizon.

Bluetooth’s Human Interface Device (HID) profile refers to the wireless communication used by peripheral devices, such as mice, keyboards, and game controllers, when connecting to a host device. Smart glasses and controllers connected to XR/VR headsets also use this profile, and their data-intensive computing will benefit greatly from HDT.

Devices using the HID profile can expect HDT to lay the foundation for making cloud gaming more reliable, spatial computing more responsive, and offloading audio/visual data from smart glasses less power-intensive.

Also: Frustrated with your Bluetooth? How multipoint works – and why it sometimes won’t

Additionally, gaming keyboards and mice can expect HDT’s expanded data processing to deliver shorter device communication intervals, reducing latency and lag.

In 2026, we’re starting to see more Bluetooth 6-equipped devices, with Samsung’s recently announced Galaxy Buds On featuring Bluetooth 6.2. Though Bluetooth’s blog post states that the first application of HDT will be for premium wireless audio, many manufacturers are behind, still releasing products with Bluetooth 5.4.

Smartphones, smartwatches, and smart home products tend to move a little faster, with the majority of current Bluetooth 6 products falling into these categories.

The Bluetooth SIG announced Auracast in 2022, and audio manufacturers are now beginning to adopt this technology more widely. Channel Sounding was released with Bluetooth 6 in 2024, with some early adopters releasing compatible products this year; more are expected next year and into 2028. With these adoption cycles in mind, we may be well into the next decade before HDT becomes standard.

15-Minute Beginner-Friendly Yoga, No Flexibility Required

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Taylor Lorenz—known online as Taylor’s Tracks—is a certified yoga teacher with more than 800 hours of training in vinyasa, hatha, and yin yoga. Here, she guides you through a practice suitable for any experience level with yoga.

No matter how long you’ve been practicing yoga, returning to the basics is always beneficial. This yoga for beginners practice features a flow full of repeating shapes, including forward bends, side stretches, and gentle twists, all supported by modifications and props, if needed, to ensure that the poses work for any body, regardless of how flexible or inflexible you might be.

Listen to your body, breathe slowly, and enjoy the flow.

Keep two yoga blocks or block-like props (think: thick books) close by. These can help you access poses that your body might otherwise object to so that you can still benefit.

Begin kneeling in Thunderbolt Pose. If this doesn’t feel comfortable for your ankles or knees, slide a block beneath your sit bones to minimize the pressure. If it’s still uncomfortable, you can skip this pose.

Come onto your hands and knees with your shoulders over your wrists, hips stacked over your knees. With your next inhalation, lower your belly, lift your chin and chest, and arch your back in Cow Pose.

Exhale as you round your back and tuck your chin in Cat Pose.

Repeat Cat and Cow 3 times each, moving with the pace of your breath.

As you round into your last Cat Pose, tuck your toes and sink your sit bones back toward your heels.

Shift your shoulders forward over your wrists. Keep your toes tucked and with your next exhalation, lift your knees away from the mat and reach your hips up and back in Downward-Facing Dog. Think of your body being in an inverted V shape.

Feel free to bend you knees as much as you need to for the pose to feel comfortable along your hamstrings and lower back. You can also place blocks underneath your hands.

Stay here for 3 cycles of breath, pedaling out your feet by bending one knee and straightening the other, and then switching. You can also sway a little from side to side or simply stay in stillness.

Walk your feet toward your hands to come into a Standing Forward Bend. Relax your neck and let your head hang. You can let your hands dangle or rest your fingertips on the mat.

You can also place a block beneath each of your hands. You might want to bend your knees slightly to ease the strain on your hamstrings or lower back.

Inhale and start to lift your chest slightly, bringing your fingertips to your shins and lengthening your spine in Halfway Lift. Keep the back of your neck long and stare straight down at the mat. You can also keep your fingertips on your blocks here.

Inhale to rise as you come to standing, sweeping your arms out toward the sides and overhead. Bring your hands together at heart center in Mountain Pose.

With your next inhalation, reach your arms back toward the sky. Grab your left wrist with your right hand and guide your left wrist up and over to the right in a Standing Side Bend. Feel the stretch along your left side body.

Inhale as you come back through center and exhale as you twist toward the right, with your left arm reaching forward and your right arm reaching toward the back of the mat. Gaze toward the right.

When you’re ready, come back through center before repeating Standing Side Bend and Standing Twist on the opposite side.

Inhale to come back through center, exhale to Standing Forward Bend.

With your next exhalation, step back to Downward-Facing Dog.

From Downward Dog, step your right foot between your hands. Lower your left knee to the mat and sweep your arms overhead in Low Lunge.

Use our right hand to grab hold of your left wrist again, guiding it up and over in a side bend while keeping your balance in your Low Lunge. Press through your right foot and your back knee to help find steadiness.

Inhale as you come back through center and exhale as you twist toward the right, with your left arm reaching forward and your right arm reaching toward the back of the mat.

Inhale both arms back to center, palms facing forward. With your exhalation, bring your hands to the ground or to blocks on either side of your front foot. Start to straighten your front leg, sliding your right heel away from you and flexing your right foot in Half Splits.

You can keep your hands underneath your shoulders or slide them forward closer to your feet for a more intense shoulder and side body stretch.

With your next inhalation, come back through Low Lunge. Exhale, Downward-Facing Dog.

Reach your left leg to the sky and repeat the following poses on the opposite side:

Step toward the front of the mat and fold forward in Standing Forward Bend.

Inhale, reaching your arms up and overhead with your big toes touching or hip-distance apart. With your exhalation, reach your arms alongside your head and sit your hips back in Chair Pose.

Keep your arms high and begin to draw your left knee toward your chest, foot flexed. With your exhalation, step your left foot back into a High Lunge.

With your right hand, grab your left wrist, and reaching up and over toward the right side.

Inhale to come back through center. Exhale to open your arms toward the right, left arm forward, right arm back.

Inhale to reach both arms toward the sky. Exhale to straighten the right leg, hinging at your hips and folding your chest toward your right leg with your back heel lifted in a less intense variation of Pyramid Pose. Bring your hands to the mat to frame your front foot, your shin, or blocks on either side of your foot. Take 1 full breath here.

When you’re ready, step your left foot up to meet your right in Standing Forward Bend.

Inhale, reaching your arms up and overhead. With your exhalation, keep your arms high and sit your hips back in Chair Pose.

Repeat the following poses on the opposite side:

Gently land your knees to your mat and move through hands and knee into a seated position. Bring the soles of your feet together and let your knees fall out toward the sides in Bound Angle Pose. If this feels too intense, slide a blocks beneath each knee for extra support.

Stay here, or round your spine and walk your hands forward as you bring your chest toward your toes. Let your body find where it wants to be rather than force the shape. Take 5 breaths in your chosen position.

When you’re ready, move through a seated position to come all the way onto your back in Savasana. Feel free to add blocks beneath your knees for extra support.

Stay here for 3-5 minutes or as long as you’d like.

Push yourself upright to a comfortable seat. Bring your hands together at heart center, bow your head, and thank yourself for showing up on your mat today.

Exoplanets Can't Hide From The JWST's 'Digital Twin'

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Exoplanets would be a lot easier to find if so many of them weren't so close to stars. But they are, and that's something exoplanet scientists have to contend with. Coronagraphs do great work blocking out starlight and making dim planets more visible, but they have their limitations.

The JWST has coronagraphic capabilities embedded in its NIRCam (Near-Infrared Camera) and MIRI (Mid-Infrared Instrument). These capabilities have helped the JWST study some tough subjects hidden in the glare of stars, like debris disks, and to observe extremely luminous objects like quasars.

But all telescopes have a stability limit, even the steady-eyed JWST. The stability limit is a threshold beyond which mechanical, thermal, or optical fluctuations make clear images impossible. The JWST has fine guidance sensors and a 5-layer sunshield to raise this threshold, but the limit is still there.

New research in Nature explains how astronomers developed a way to subtract more starlight from the JWST's observations and image exoplanets that are up against the telescope's limits. It's titled "Imaging exoplanets below the telescope stability limit," and the lead author is Rodrigo Ferrer-Chavez. Ferrero-Chavez is a graduate student in the Department of Physics & Astronomy at Northwestern University.

"Exoplanet imaging is the main method for detailed characterization of planets orbiting beyond 1âau (astronomical unit) but it requires suppressing the bright glare of the star to see the faint planet light," the authors write. Suppressing that glare requires a couple of things, including extremely stable telescopes.

âWe try to make telescopes extremely stable, but there are extreme engineering challenges that we havenât quite solved yet,â said study co-author Jason Wang, assistant professor of physics and astronomy at Weinberg College of Arts and Sciences at NW. âThere are known causes of instability and unknown causes. Small thermal changes could cause a mirror to expand or contract by a picometer. Telescopes like JWST have cryocoolers to keep the instrument super cold. Those coolers have little motors that can cause the mirrors to vibrate. So, there are issues like those but also issues that we still donât understand as we learn how to build these complex telescopes.â

But a stable telescope is only part of the picture. Image processing plays a huge role in modern astronomy, and the two factors, telescope stability and image processing, play into each other.

"As well as sophisticated instrumentation, effective image processing is necessary for starlight suppression, which continues to be a substantial limitation," the authors write.

There are standard algorithms that astronomers employ to overcome this limitation, including using reference images to model starlight.

âFor the past 20 years, astronomers have tackled this problem by observing a different star and using its image as a reference,â said Wang said. âThen, they subtract that reference image from the original observation to remove the unwanted glare to reveal faint planets that otherwise might be hidden.â

This image shows how the JWST uses coronagraphic masks in MIRI and NIRCam to remove starlight. The white star marks the location of the real star and illustrates how it's been effectively removed from the image. It's effective, but the new method will increase its effectiveness and reveal even dimmer exoplanets. Image Credit: NASA, ESA, CSA, Alyssa Pagan (STScI); Science: ERS 1386 Team, Aarynn Carter (UC Santa Cruz)

But reference images have their limits too. That method still suffers from inevitable instabilities in a telescope that arise when switching from a reference image to an actual observation. Overcoming these stubborn obstacles will mean we can directly image more exoplanets.

"Addressing this challenge will increase the number of planets amenable to direct imaging and will be a notable step towards imaging exo-Earths with future observatories," the researchers explain.

Their answer is a new method based on physics and modelling that also borrows ideas from machine-learning. It can account for the JWST's tiny, nanoscale imperfections. The method lets astronomers subtract starlight from images more effectively than ever before.

âPlanets are a million times fainter than their stars,â said lead author Ferrer-Chavez. âSo, to image exoplanets, we need to remove that glare. That is a very tricky problem because even the tiniest changes in the telescope can affect how that glare appears. Slight shifts or tilts â even on the scale of just a few nanometers â can change the pattern of light. Our method models how light travels through the telescope and accounts for those tiny changes, allowing us to remove the glare much more precisely.â

This comes down to PSF, point spread function. Every telescope detects light imperfectly. They all distort or bend light in some way. So instead of a point source appearing as a point, it inevitably is degraded somehow. That can hide extremely dim sources like exoplanets in the glare of their stars. Accounting for PSF is a work-a-day part of astronomy.

The new system is called FLIP, (Physics-based Learning for Imaging Planets) and it overcomes the problem of switching from reference images to real observations. FLIP "incorporates a multistep optimization strategy with a flexible physical model informed by wavefront sensing data to achieve the PSF fidelity necessary for real data," the authors write.

That's more technical detail than many will find interesting. But this type of work is behind the gorgeous images and fascinating discoveries in astronomy.

(a) shows the usual method of subtracting starlight from observations using reference images. Those image are imperfect and when combined with telescope stability, faint exoplanets remain hidden. (b) shows FLIP. Rather than reference images, it uses a differentiable instrument model and a physics based starlight model rather than an empirical starlight model. That means that starlight is more effectively removed from the end image, and a faint exoplanet becomes visible. Hooray! Image Credit: Ferrer-Chavez, R., Wang, J.J., Bouman, K.L. et al. Imaging exoplanets below the telescope stability limit. Nature 659, 58â62 (2026).

In essence, the researchers created what they're calling a "digital twin" of the JWST with FLIP. The twin reproduces how light travels through the JWST down to the smallest detail. It includes subtle shifts, distortions, and imperfections. They repeatedly adjusted FLIP until it matched the JWST's real observations, including the polluting starlight. After that, they subtracted the star's glare from the image.

They tested it on 51 Eridani b, an exoplanet found with ground-based telescopes. The usual image processing techniques couldn't see the exoplanet hidden in the glare when observed at the wavelength that the JWST uses to find CO2 in exoplanet atmospheres. (They chose that wavelength because that's a critical part of the JWST's job, and a sort of sweet spot in its capabilities.)

But when the researchers used FLIP, the planet came into view.

Left: JWST/NIRCam observation of star 51 Eridani A (51 Eri) in the F430M filter, using a standard processing method named KLIPto subtract the glare of the star. This method was not able to detect the planet 51 Eri b in the original analysis, whose expected location is marked with a dashed circle. Middle: The same observation, but using new method FLIP to subtract the glare of the star. Astrophysicists obtained a significant detection (>5 sigma) of 51 Eri b. Right: Simulated image of 51 Eri b, highlighting that the planet is detected at its expected location and that the planet signal has the expected morphology. The location of the star 51 Eri is marked with a star symbol. Image credit: Rodrigo Ferrer-Chavez.

"We achieve sensitivity 2â15 times superior to standard techniques, including the first detection of 51âEridaniâb at 4.3âÎ¼m," the authors write. "This is the first time, to our knowledge, that such an approach has demonstrated substantially improved sensitivity on real, on-sky coronagraphic imaging."

âWe showed that we can actually recover a planet that people previously couldnât see,â Wang said. âThis is real data in a real scenario. It was science left on the table because we didnât have the right tools to analyze the data in the most optimal way.â

FLIP seems to have a bright future. Not only with the JWST, but with other space telescopes like the Habitable Worlds Observatory. Imaging exo-Earths with the HWO, for example, will require extreme stability, and FLIP might get us there. In fact, FLIP might make the HWO more easy to design and build.

"In this work, we have shown sensitivity beyond the stability limit in JWSTâNIRCam," the authors write. "If this can be extended to HWO contrast levels, the ultrastability requirement for the system may be partially lifted, with the error budget necessary for image post-processing being reduced, and thus the design of the observatory may become more tractable."

In short, FLIP can take some of the pressure off of the engineering challenges in building more and more powerful telescopes, and can help overcome their stability limits.

âWe know of no planets around other stars that are like those in our own solar system,â Ferrer-Chavez said. âItâs not that they donât exist. Itâs that we cannot see them. Every time we improve our techniques, we discover new types of planets. By finding more planets, we can better understand how planetary systems form and evolve, and ultimately how typical our own solar system truly is.â

Evan Gough is a science-loving guy with no formal education who loves Earth, forests, hiking, and heavy music. He's guided by Carl Sagan's quote: "Understanding is a kind of ecstasy."

« Le jour est venu de rendre le numéro 7 » : Cristiano Ronaldo annonce sa retraite internationale !

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Ce jeudi 5 novembre 2026, Cristiano Ronaldo a officialisé sa retraite internationale avec le Portugal à travers une vidéo d'environ cinq minutes et une lettre d'adieu publiées sur ses réseaux sociaux. Âgé de 41 ans, l'attaquant met un terme à une aventure de 23 ans débutée en août 2003 avec la Seleção das Quinas.

Dans sa lettre adressée aux supporters portugais, Cristiano Ronaldo a expliqué que le jour était venu de rendre le maillot numéro 7 et de le transmettre aux générations futures, précisant qu'il s'agissait d'un choix dicté tant par le cœur que par la tête. L'attaquant a également rendu hommage à ses coéquipiers, à ses entraîneurs, à l'ensemble du staff technique et médical, ainsi qu'à sa famille et aux supporters portugais à travers le monde. La fin d'une ère pour la sélection.

Au-delà de ces explications, le joueur a tenu à saluer la ferveur constante du public qui l'a accompagné pendant plus de deux décennies. L'annonce de son départ a d'ailleurs immédiatement suscité l'émotion de son ancien coéquipier Pepe. Ce dernier a ainsi tenu à saluer son parcours sur les réseaux sociaux en commentant la publication : « C'était bien plus que du football. Merci pour tout, Cris ».

Cette annonce vient clore une aventure débutée en août 2003 avec la Seleção das Quinas. En 23 ans de carrière en sélection, Cristiano Ronaldo s'est constitué un bilan sans précédent sur la scène internationale. Il détient en effet le record mondial masculin de sélections et de buts en équipe nationale avec 146 réalisations en 234 matches. Un bilan exceptionnel. Au cours de cette épopée de 23 ans sous le maillot portugais, l'attaquant a marqué l'histoire de son pays.

Avec la sélection portugaise, Cristiano Ronaldo a ainsi remporté l'Euro 2016 ainsi que deux Ligues des nations (2018-2019 et 2024-2025). Toutefois, cette retraite ne concerne que la scène internationale. En effet, le joueur de 41 ans ne met pas un terme à sa carrière sur les terrains et poursuit son parcours en club avec Al-Nassr.

Cette annonce survient dans la foulée de tensions survenues lors du rassemblement de septembre 2026. L'attaquant avait alors quitté la sélection après avoir passé l'intégralité du match sur le banc sans entrer en jeu contre la Norvège sous les ordres de Jorge Jesus. Malgré cet épisode houleux et une sanction de 30 jours purgée, Cristiano Ronaldo avait tout de même reçu une pré-convocation pour le rassemblement de novembre avant de choisir de raccrocher définitivement en sélection.

D'ailleurs, la Fédération portugaise de football a publié un communiqué pour saluer son capitaine emblématique et a annoncé qu'un hommage officiel digne de son immense carrière lui serait prochainement rendu. Désormais, l'équipe nationale doit se projeter sur la suite de la compétition. Le Portugal disputera sa prochaine rencontre officielle le samedi 14 novembre 2026 à 20h45 face au Danemark à l'Estádio Municipal de Braga en Ligue des nations.

Hunger Games Fans, Did You Spot These 12 Hidden Plant Meanings in Sunrise on the Reaping?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

7 Underrated Perennials to Plant in October – For The Gorgeous Fall Color You Never Knew You Were Missing
